The discrete homotopy hypothesis for directed graphs
Briony Eldridge, Sergei O. Ivanov, Xiaomeng Xu, Shing-Tung Yau, Mengmeng Zhang

Abstract
. We develop a homotopy theory of directed graphs based on cubical homotopy groups—also referred to as A A -groups or reduced GLMY homotopy groups. Localizing the category of directed graphs at morphisms that induce isomorphisms on these groups yields an ∞ \infty -category, which we denote by 𝖣𝖦𝗋𝖺 ∞ {\sf DGra}_{\infty} . Our main result shows that 𝖣𝖦𝗋𝖺 ∞ {\sf DGra}_{\infty} is equivalent to the ∞ \infty -category of spaces.



Introduction

A homotopy theory of (simple) graphs was introduced in
[
BKLW01
,
BL05
,
BBdLL06
]
, building on earlier ideas of Atkin
[
Atk74
,
Atk76
]
. The central invariant of this theory is the A-group of a graph, denoted by
A_{n}(G)
, which is an analogue of the
n
-th homotopy group of a topological space. In
[
BBdLL06
]
, it was conjectured that
A_{n}(G)
is isomorphic to the
n
-th homotopy group of a certain topological space. This conjecture was proved by Carranza and Kapulkin in
[
CK24
]
, where they constructed a functor to the category of cubical sets

N_{1}:\mathsf{Gra}\longrightarrow\mathsf{cSet}

called the cubical nerve, and showed that
A_{n}(G)\cong\pi_{n}(|N_{1}G|)
.

Independently, a homotopy theory of digraphs (directed graphs) was introduced in
[
GLMY23
]
and further developed in
[
LWYZ24
,
TWYZ25
]
. This theory is closely related to the path homology theory, also known as the GLMY-theory, developed earlier in
[
GLMY12
]
. This theory also includes an analogue of homotopy groups, denoted by
\bar{\pi}_{n}(G)
. These groups extend A-groups to digraphs and also admit a description in terms of cubical sets. For this reason we refer to these groups as the cubical homotopy groups of
G
and denote them by
A_{n}(G)
. Digraph maps that induce isomorphisms on cubical homotopy groups are called cubical weak equivalences.

In
[
CK26
]
, Carranza and Kapulkin formulated the following
discrete homotopy hypothesis
. Let
\mathsf{Gra}_{\infty}
denote the (
\infty
-categorical) localization of the category of graphs
\mathsf{Gra}
at the class of cubical weak equivalences. Then the cubical nerve induces a functor to the
\infty
-category of spaces

N:\mathsf{Gra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}.

The discrete homotopy hypothesis asserts that this functor is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories.
This conjecture remains open, although several partial results have been obtained; see
[
KM24
,
Min26
,
CK26
]
. As explained in
[
CK26
]
, the principal obstruction to the original discrete homotopy hypothesis is that pushouts of graphs admit a large number of non-degenerate cubes that are difficult to control. This issue can be resolved by passing to the category of digraphs, where the presence of directions imposes sufficient constraints to control the newly generated cubes arising in pushouts.

Therefore, in this paper, we study an analogue of the discrete homotopy hypothesis for digraphs. First we extend the results of
[
CK24
]
to the setting of digraphs. In particular, we construct the cubical nerve functor
N_{1}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
and prove that
A_{n}(G)\cong\pi_{n}(|N_{1}G|)
. Let
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
denote the
\infty
-category obtained from
\mathsf{DGra}
by localizing at the class of cubical weak equivalences. Then the functor
N_{1}
induces a functor to the
\infty
-category of spaces
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
. Our main result is the following.

Theorem

(Th.
5.5.3
)
.

The functor
N
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\simeq\mathsf{Spc}.

As a corollary we obtain that for every pointed space
X
there exists a pointed digraph
G
such that
A_{*}(G)\cong\pi_{*}(X)
.

We further describe finite limits and small colimits in
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
by equipping
\mathsf{DGra}
with structures of a category of fibrant objects and a homotopy cocomplete category of cofibrant objects respectively. As an application, we recover the long exact sequence of cubical homotopy groups constructed in
[
LWYZ24
]
(Corollary
3.3.3
).

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
1
, we develop a homotopy theory of digraphs, where homotopies are defined by digraph maps
G\otimes I_{1}\to H
, and introduce the cubical homotopy groups
A_{*}(G)
. We consider the
category of shrinkings

\mathsf{Shr}
, whose objects are intervals and morphisms are shrinkings. We define a notion of a
nice subcategory of shrinkings

\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathsf{Shr}
and show that
A_{*}(G)
admits an equivalent definition in terms of any such
\mathbf{S}
. This provides a unified framework encompassing several approaches in the literature. We also define the path and loop digraphs
P_{\mathbf{S}}G
and
\Omega_{\mathbf{S}}G
respectively, depending on
\mathbf{S}
, and prove that they satisfy the expected properties whenever
\mathbf{S}
is cofiltered.

In Section
2
, generalizing the ideas of
[
CK24
]
, for any
m\geq 1
, we introduce a functor
N_{m}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
, called cubical
m
-nerve. For any digraph
G
, we consider the colimit of a sequence

N_{1}G\to N_{2}G\to N_{4}G\to N_{6}G\to\dots

that defines a functor
N_{\infty}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}.

For technical reasons specific to the directed setting, it is convenient to work with the sequence starting at
N_{1}G
and then passing through the even-indexed terms
N_{2m}G
. We extend the results of
[
CK24
]
by proving that all maps in this sequence are anodyne, and
N_{\infty}G
is a Kan complex, and
A_{*}(G)\cong\pi_{*}(N_{\infty}G)
. In particular, the functors
N_{1}
,
N_{2m}
, and
N_{\infty}
induce the same functor of
\infty
-categories

N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}.

Furthermore, we show that
NG
can be described as a mapping space from the one-point digraph in the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
:

NG\cong{\mathsf{DGra}}_{\infty}(*,G).

In Section
3
, we introduce the notion of a cubical fibration of digraphs and show that
\mathsf{DGra}
, together with the classes of cubical weak equivalences and cubical fibrations, forms a category of fibrant objects. This framework allows us to describe finite limits in
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
and to prove the long exact sequence of cubical homotopy groups. To illustrate the notion of cubical fibration, we demonstrate that the 2-coverings introduced in
[
DIMZ24
]
are cubical fibrations.

In Section
4
, we introduce the notion of an in-closed subdigraph and prove a nerve theorem for covers of a digraph by in-closed subdigraphs. This provides a useful tool for computing the homotopy type of
NG
for certain classes of digraphs
G
.

In Section
5
, we introduce the notion of an in-closed cofibration and show that the category
\mathsf{DGra}
, equipped with the classes of cubical weak equivalences and in-closed cofibrations, is a homotopy cocomplete category of cofibrant objects. Moreover, we prove that
N_{1}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
is a homotopy cocontinuous functor. Consequently,
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
admits small colimits and
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
is a colimit-preserving functor. By the universal property of the
\infty
-category of spaces
\mathsf{Spc}
, we obtain a colimit-preserving functor

R:\mathsf{Spc}\longrightarrow\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}

satisfying
R(*)=*
. We then show that
R
and
N
are mutually inverse equivalences.

1.
Cubical homotopy groups

This section provides a preliminary overview of the ordinary homotopy of digraphs, where homotopy is defined as a map
G\otimes I_{1}\to H
. We define the category of digraphs
\mathsf{DGra}
, its homotopy category
\mathsf{DGra}/\sim
, the cubical homotopy groups
A_{*}(G)
, and the path and loop digraphs. While versions of this theory appear in the literature (see
[
CK24
,
GLMY23
,
LWYZ24
]
), our primary contribution introduces the concept of a nice subcategory of shrinkings
\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathsf{Shr}
, where
\mathsf{Shr}
is the category of intervals and their “shrinkings”. We show that the cubical homotopy groups
A_{*}(G)
can be defined using any nice subcategory of shrinkings
\mathbf{S}
, yielding many equivalent definitions. We also define the path digraph
P_{\mathbf{S}}G
and the loop digraph
\Omega_{\mathbf{S}}G
relative to a specific
\mathbf{S}
. We prove that these digraphs satisfy all expected properties provided that
\mathbf{S}
is cofiltered.

1.1.
Homotopy category of digraphs

In this subsection we give some basic definitions and results related to the category of digraphs, the category of pairs of digraphs and their homotopy categories. We give them without proofs because the proofs are straightforward. Many of the statements in this subsection are direct generalizations of statements from
[
CK24
,
GLMY23
,
LWYZ24
]
.

Definition 1.1.1

(Quivers)
.

Let
{\sf q}
be the category consisting of two objects
[0]=\{0\}
and
[1]=\{0,1\}
and all monotone maps between them. This category is generated by morphisms

d^{0},d^{1}:[0]\to[1]
,
s^{0}:[1]\to[0],

where
d^{0}(0)=1
,
d^{1}(0)=0
and
s^{0}
is the only map from
[1]
to
[0]
. They satisfy relations

s^{0}d^{0}=\mathrm{id}_{\{0\}}=s^{0}d^{1}.

The category of quivers is defined as the category of presheaves over
{\sf q}

(1.1.1)

\mathsf{Quiv}=\mathrm{Fun}({\sf q}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{Set}).

It is easy to see that a quiver
Q
is defined by two sets

V(Q):=Q([0])
and
E(Q):=Q([1])

called the set of vertices and the set of arrows, and three maps

(1.1.2)

d_{0},d_{1}:E(Q)\to V(Q),\hskip 28.45274pts_{0}:V(Q)\to E(Q)

satisfying the relations

d_{0}s_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{V(Q)}=d_{1}s_{0}.
For
v\in V(Q)
, the arrow
s_{0}(v)\in E(Q)
is called degenerate arrow and it is not depicted in a picture of a quiver.

Remark 1.1.2

((Co)limits of quivers)
.

Since
\mathsf{Quiv}
is a category of presheaves over a small category, it is complete and cocomplete, and the limits and colimits are computed objectwise.

Definition 1.1.3

(Digraphs)
.

A digraph
G
is a quiver such that
E(G)\subseteq V(G)\times V(G)
and
d_{0},d_{1},s_{0}
are defined by formulas
d_{1}(g,h)=g,

d_{0}(g,h)=h
and
s_{0}(g)=(g,g).
The arrows of the form
(g,g)
are called degenerate arrows and they are not depicted in a picture of a digraph. The full subcategory of the category of quivers spanned by digraphs is denoted by

(1.1.3)

\mathsf{DGra}\subseteq\mathsf{Quiv}.

By an abuse of notation, we write
g\in G
to denote a vertex of
G
, rather than the more formal
g\in V(G)
.

Proposition 1.1.4
.

The subcategory
\mathsf{DGra}\subseteq\mathsf{Quiv}
is reflective. The reflector

(1.1.4)

{\sf d}:\mathsf{Quiv}\longrightarrow\mathsf{DGra}

is defined so that
V({\sf d}(Q))=V(Q)
and

E({\sf d}(Q))
is defined as the image of the map
(d_{1},d_{0}):E(Q)\to V(Q)\times V(Q).

Corollary 1.1.5

((Co)limits of digraphs)
.

The category
\mathsf{DGra}
is complete and cocomplete.
\mathsf{DGra}
is closed with respect to limits in
\mathsf{Quiv}
. In particular, for any functor
F:I\to\mathsf{DGra}
, we have

(1.1.5)

\lim F\cong{\sf d}(\lim iF).

Colimits in
\mathsf{DGra}
are obtained as colimits in
\mathsf{Quiv}
followed by application of
{\sf d}
. More precisely, for a functor
F:I\to\mathsf{DGra}
, we have

(1.1.6)

\mathrm{colim}\>F={\sf d}(\mathrm{colim}\>iF),

where
i:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{Quiv}
is the inclusion.

Proposition 1.1.6
.

In
\mathsf{DGra}
, filtered colimits commute with finite limits.

Definition 1.1.7

(Opposite digraph)
.

For a digraph
G
, we denote by
G^{\mathrm{op}}
the digraph with the same set of vertices and the opposite directions of arrows

E(G^{\mathrm{op}})={\sf tw}(E(G)),

where
{\sf tw}:V(G)^{2}\to V(G)^{2}
is defined by
{\sf tw}(g,g^{\prime})=(g^{\prime},g)
.

Definition 1.1.8

(Graphs)
.

A graph
G
is a digraph such that
G=G^{\mathrm{op}}
. The category of graphs is the full subcategory of digraphs

(1.1.7)

\mathsf{Gra}\subseteq\mathsf{DGra}.

Proposition 1.1.9
.

The subcategory
\mathsf{Gra}\subseteq\mathsf{DGra}
is reflective. The reflector

(1.1.8)

{\sf g}:\mathsf{DGra}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Gra}

is defined by the formulas
V({\sf g}(G))=V(G)
and

E({\sf g}(G))=E(G)\cup E(G^{\mathrm{op}}).

Remark 1.1.10

(Colimits of graphs)
.

The subcategory
\mathsf{Gra}\subseteq\mathsf{DGra}
is closed with respect to limits and colimits.

Definition 1.1.11

(Induced subdigraph)
.

A subquiver of a quiver is a sub-presheaf of a quiver. A subdigraph of a digraph is a subquiver of a digraph. A subdigraph
H\subseteq G
is called induced if any arrow of
G
between vertices of
H
is an arrow of
H
.

Remark 1.1.12

(Pushout along an inclusion of an induced subdigraph)
.

Let
H\subseteq G
be an induced subdigraph and
\varphi:H\to H^{\prime}
be a digraph map. Then the pushout
G^{\prime}=G\sqcup_{H}H^{\prime}
can be described as the commutative diagram

(1.1.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}

where set of vertices
V(G^{\prime})
is defined by

(1.1.10)

V(G^{\prime})=(V(G)\setminus V(H))\cup V(H^{\prime}).

The map
\varphi^{\prime}:V(G)\to V(G^{\prime})
is defined by
\varphi^{\prime}(g)=g
for
g\in V(G)\setminus V(H)
and
\varphi^{\prime}(h)=\varphi(h)
for
h\in V(H)
. The set of arrows is defined as

(1.1.11)

E(G^{\prime})=(\varphi^{\prime}\times\varphi^{\prime})(E(G))\cup E(H^{\prime}).

As a corollary of this description of a pushout along an inclusion of an induced subdigraph we obtain an isomorphism

(1.1.12)

G^{\prime}\setminus H^{\prime}\cong G\setminus H.

Definition 1.1.13

(Box product)
.

Let
G,H
be digraphs. The box-product
G\otimes H
is a digraph such that
V(G\otimes H)=V(G)\times V(H)
and
E(G\otimes H)
is the image of the map

(1.1.13)

(E(G)\times V(H))\underset{V(G)\times V(H)}{\coprod}(V(G)\times E(H))\to(V(G)\times V(H))^{2}.

induced by the inclusions

(1.1.14)

E(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow V(\Gamma)\times V(\Gamma),\hskip 28.45274pt{\sf diag}:V(\Gamma)\to V(\Gamma)\times V(\Gamma)

for
\Gamma=G,H,
and the twist map
V(G)^{2}\times V(H)^{2}\to(V(G)\times V(H))^{2}
. In other words, there is an arrow
(g,h)\to(g^{\prime},h^{\prime})
in
G\otimes H
if and only if either there is an arrow
g\to g^{\prime}
and
h=h^{\prime}
, or there is an arrow
h\to h^{\prime}
and
g=g^{\prime}.
The box product is natural in
G
and
H.
Note that the box product is a subdigraph of the product

(1.1.15)

G\otimes H\subseteq G\times H.

Definition 1.1.14

(Box hom)
.

The box hom of two digraphs
G
and
H
is defined as the digraph
\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(G,H)
whose set of vertices is
\mathrm{Hom}(G,H)
and for any two morphisms
\varphi,\psi:G\to H
there is an arrow
\varphi\to\psi
in
\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(G,H)
if and only if there is an arrow
\varphi(g)\to\psi(g)
in
H
for any
g\in G.
The box hom is natural in
G
and
H
.

Proposition 1.1.15
.

(\mathsf{DGra},*,\otimes,\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes})
form a closed monoidal category, where
*
is the one-point digraph.

Definition 1.1.16

(Connected components)
.

For a digraph
G
, we denote by
\pi_{0}(G)
the set of its connected components.

Definition 1.1.17

(Homotopy relation)
.

We say that two maps of digraphs
\varphi,\psi:G\to H
are homotopic, denoted by
\varphi\sim\psi
, if they are in the same connected component of
\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(G,H).
The quotient set is denoted by

(1.1.16)

[G,H]=\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(G,H)).

Proposition 1.1.18
.

The homotopy relation is a congruence relation on the category of digraphs
\mathsf{DGra}.
Therefore we can consider the homotopy category

(1.1.17)

\mathsf{DGra}/\sim.

Definition 1.1.19

(Pairs of digraphs and pointed digraphs)
.

A pair of digraphs is a pair
(G,H),
where
H\subseteq G
is an induced subdigraph. A morphism of pairs of digraphs
\varphi:(G,H)\to(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
is a morphism
\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
such that
\varphi(H)\subseteq H^{\prime}.
The category of pairs of digraphs is denoted by
\mathsf{PairDGra}.
It can be viewed as a full subcategory of the category of morphisms of digraphs

(1.1.18)

\mathsf{PairDGra}\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathsf{DGra}),

where
[1]=\{0<1\}
is the category with two objects and one non-identity morphism. A pointed digraph is a pair of digraphs, where the subdigraph consists of one
vertex

(G,g)=(G,\{g\}).

The full subcategory of pointed digraphs is denoted by

(1.1.19)

\mathsf{DGra}_{*}\subseteq\mathsf{PairDGra}.

Definition 1.1.20

(Box product of pairs)
.

The box product of pairs of digraphs
(G,H)
and
(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
is defined by

(1.1.20)

(G,H)\otimes(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})=(G\otimes G^{\prime},(G\otimes H^{\prime})\cup(H\otimes G^{\prime})).

Definition 1.1.21

(Box hom of pairs)
.

The box hom between pairs of digraphs
(G,H)
and
(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
is a digraph
\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}((G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime}))
whose set of vertices is
\mathrm{Hom}((G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime}))
and for maps
\varphi,\psi:(G,H)\to(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
there is an arrow
\varphi\to\psi
if and only if for any
g\in G
there is an arrow
\varphi(g)\to\psi(g)
, and for any
h\in H
we have
\varphi(h)=\psi(h).

We denote by
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}^{\otimes}((G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime}))
a pair of digraphs

(1.1.21)

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}^{\otimes}((G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime}))=(\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}((G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})),\mathrm{Hom}(G,H^{\prime})),

where
\mathrm{Hom}(G,H^{\prime})
is the induced subdigraph of
\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}((G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime}))
consisting of vertices as maps
G\to G^{\prime}
whose image lies in
H^{\prime}
. If
G=(G,g)
and
G^{\prime}=(G^{\prime},g^{\prime})
are pointed digraphs, we set

(1.1.22)

\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}_{*}(G,G^{\prime})=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}^{\otimes}(G,G^{\prime}).

Proposition 1.1.22
.

(\mathsf{PairDGra},(*,\emptyset),\otimes,\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}^{\otimes})
is a closed monoidal category.

Definition 1.1.23

(Relative homotopy relation)
.

Two maps of pairs
\varphi,\psi:(G,H)\to(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
are called homotopic relative to
H
, denoted by
\varphi\sim_{\sf rel}\psi
if they are in the same connected component of
\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}((G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})).

The quotient-set is denoted by

(1.1.23)

[(G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})]=\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}((G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime}))).

Proposition 1.1.24
.

The relative homotopy relation is a congruence on
\mathsf{PairDGra}
. Therefore we can consider the homotopy category of pairs

(1.1.24)

\mathsf{PairDGra}/\sim_{\sf rel}.

Proposition 1.1.25
.

For any three pairs of digraphs
(G,H),

(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
and
(G^{\prime\prime},H^{\prime\prime})
, there is a natural isomorphism

(1.1.25)

[(G,H)\otimes(G^{\prime},H^{\prime}),(G^{\prime\prime},H^{\prime\prime})]\cong[(G,H),\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}^{\otimes}((G^{\prime},H^{\prime}),(G^{\prime\prime},H^{\prime\prime}))].

Definition 1.1.26

(Interval)
.

An interval is a digraph
J
with the vertices
\{0,\dots,k\},
that has exactly
k
non-degenerate arrows: for each
0\leq i<k
either
i\to i+1
or
i+1\to i
is an arrow.

(1.1.26)

J:0\to 1\to 2\leftarrow 3\to 4\to 5\leftarrow 6

If
k=0
, the interval is called trivial.
For any interval
J
, we denote by
\partial J
the discrete digraph defined by the endpoints
\{0,k\}.
We also denote by
s,t:*\to J
the inclusion of end-points
s(*)=0
and
t(*)=k
. For any two intervals
J
and
J^{\prime}
with the sets of vertices
\{0,\dots,k\}
and
\{0,\dots,k^{\prime}\}
we denote by

J\vee J^{\prime}

their “wedge” i.e. the interval with the set of points
\{0,\dots,k+k^{\prime}\}
and the set of edges
E(J)\cup(E(J^{\prime})+(k,k))
, where
E(J^{\prime})+(k,k)=\{(i+k,j+k)\mid(i,j)\in E(J^{\prime})\}
.

Remark 1.1.27

(Homotopy relation via intervals)
.

Two digraph maps
\varphi,\psi:G\to G^{\prime}
are homotopic if and only if there is an interval
J
with the set of vertices
\{0,\dots,k\}
and a map
\eta:G\otimes J\to G^{\prime}
such that
\eta(g,0)=\varphi(g)
and
\eta(g,k)=\psi(g)
for any
g\in G
. The map
\eta
is called a homotopy from
\varphi
to
\psi
.

If
H\subseteq G
and
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
are induced subdigraphs, then
\varphi,\psi:(G,H)\to(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
are homotopic relative to
H
if and only if there exists a homotopy
\eta:G\otimes J\to G^{\prime}
from
\varphi
to
\psi
such that
\varphi(h)=\eta(h,j)=\psi(h)
for any
h\in H
and
j\in J.

Lemma 1.1.28
.

For any given non-trivial interval
J
, and any two pairs of digraphs
(G,H)
and
(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
the set
[(G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime})]
is the equalizer of two maps

(1.1.27)

\mathrm{Hom}((G\otimes J,H\otimes J),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime}))\rightrightarrows\mathrm{Hom}((G,H),(G^{\prime},H^{\prime}))

induced by
s,t:*\to J.

Proposition 1.1.29
.

Let
(G,H)
be a pair of finite digraphs. Then
(G,H)
is a compact object of
\mathsf{PairDGra}
and a compact object of
\mathsf{PairDGra}/\sim_{\sf rel}.

1.2.
Shrinkings

In this subsection, we introduce the category of shrinkings. Its objects are intervals (Definition
1.1.26
), and its morphisms are certain digraph maps between intervals called shrinkings. We will be interested not only in the category of shrinkings itself, but also in its various subcategories. To unify the subsequent theory, we introduce the notion of a nice subcategory of shrinkings and prove some of its properties.

Definition 1.2.1

(Category of shrinkings)
.

A shrinking is a morphism of intervals
J\to J^{\prime}
which is surjective and monotone on vertices
[
GLMY23
, Def.4.4]
. It is easy to see that shrinkings are closed with respect to composition. The category of all intervals and shrinkings is denoted by

\mathsf{Shr}

and called the category of shrinkings.

Definition 1.2.2

(Homotopy category of shrinkings)
.

The category of shrinkings can be treated as a subcategory of the category of pairs of digraphs

(1.2.1)

\mathsf{Shr}\subseteq\mathsf{PairDGra}

whose objects are pairs
(J,\partial J)
and morphisms are shrinkings. Then the image of
\mathsf{Shr}
in
\mathsf{PairDGra}/\sim_{\sf rel}
is called the homotopy category of shrinkings and denoted by

(1.2.2)

{\sf HShr}\subseteq{\sf PairDGra}/\sim_{\sf rel}.

Its objects are intervals and morphisms are shrinkings
J\to J^{\prime}
up to homotopy relative to
\partial J
.

Proposition 1.2.3
.

Any two shrinkings
J\to J^{\prime}
are homotopic relative to
\partial J.
In other words, the homotopy category of shrinkings
{\sf HShr}
is a posetal category.

Proof.

Let
J
and
J^{\prime}
be two intervals with sets of vertices
\{0,\dots,n\}
and
\{0,\dots,m\}
and sets of arrows
E=E(J)
and
E^{\prime}=E(J^{\prime}).
We prove that any two shrinkings
J\to J^{\prime}
are homotopic relative to
\partial J.
The proof is by induction on
n+m.

The base of induction
n+m=0
is obvious. The cases
n<m
and
m=0
are also obvious, so we assume that
n\geq m\geq 1.
We denote by
\bar{J}\subseteq J
and
\bar{J}^{\prime}\subseteq J^{\prime}
sub-intervals with the sets of vertices
\{0,\dots,n-1\}
and
\{0,\dots,m-1\}
respectively. If the directions of the last arrows are different,
(n-1,n)\in E
and
(m,m-1)\in E^{\prime}
or
(n,n-1)\in E
and
(m-1,m)\in E^{\prime}
, then any shrinking
J\to J^{\prime}
factors via a shrinking
\bar{J}\to J^{\prime}.
In this case the result follows from the inductive assumption.

Now assume that the directions of the last arrows are the same. Without loss of generality assume the last arrows are
(n-1,n)\in E
and
(m-1,m)\in E^{\prime}.
Then there are shrinkings
s:J\to J^{\prime}
of two types:

(1)

s(n-1)=m-1
;

(2)

s(n-1)=m
.

Shrinkings of the first type are in bijection with shrinkings
\bar{J}\to\bar{J}^{\prime}.
Shrinkings of the second type are in bijection with shrinkings
\bar{J}\to J^{\prime}.
Therefore, by the inductive assumption all shrinkings of the first type are homotopic to each other modulo
\partial J
, and all shrinkings of the second type are homotopic to each other modulo
\partial J.
If one of the two sets is empty, there is nothing to prove. So we assume that both of them are not empty. Therefore, we need to show that a shrinking of the second type is homotopic to a shrinking of the first type relative to
\partial J.

Take a shrinking of the second type
s:J\to J^{\prime}
. Then
s(n-1)=m.
Consider a map
s^{\prime}:\{0,\dots,n\}\to\{0,\dots,m\}
defined by

(1.2.3)

s^{\prime}(x)=\begin{cases}s(x),&s(x)<m\\
m-1,&x\neq n,s(x)=m,\\
m,&x=n.\\
\end{cases}

We claim that
s^{\prime}
is a shrinking of the first type which is homotopic to
s
relative to
\partial J.
We first verify that
s^{\prime}
is a digraph map. For an arrow
(x,y)\in E
such that
s(x),s(y)<m
, we have
(s^{\prime}(x),s^{\prime}(y))=(s(x),s(y))\in E^{\prime}.
If
(x,y)\in E
such that
s(x)<m
and
s(y)=m
, then
s(x)=m-1.
In this case either
(s^{\prime}(x),s^{\prime}(y))=(m-1,m-1)\in E^{\prime}
or
(s^{\prime}(x),s^{\prime}(y))=(m-1,m)\in E^{\prime}
. Since
(m-1,m)\in E
, the case that
(x,y)\in E
and
s(x)=m
and
s(y)<m
is impossible. If
(x,y)\in E
such that
s(x)=s(y)=m,
we have either
(s^{\prime}(x),s^{\prime}(y))=(m-1,m-1)\in E^{\prime}
, or
(s^{\prime}(x),s^{\prime}(y))=(m-1,m)\in E^{\prime}
or
(s^{\prime}(x),s^{\prime}(y))=(m,m)\in E
.

In order to check that
s
and
s^{\prime}
are homotopic relative to
\partial J,
we just note that for any
0\leq x\leq n,
there is an arrow
s^{\prime}(x)\to s(x),
and that
s^{\prime}(0)=s(0)
and
s^{\prime}(n)=s(n)
.
∎

Proposition 1.2.4
.

The posetal category
{\sf H\mathsf{Shr}}
is cofiltered.

Proof.

It follows from the fact that for any two intervals
J
and
J^{\prime}
there are shrinkings
J\vee J^{\prime}\to J
and
J\vee J^{\prime}\to J^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 1.2.5
.

The category
\mathsf{Shr}
is not cofiltered. Indeed, for any two different shrinkings
s_{1},s_{2}:J^{\prime}\to J,
there is no a shrinking
s:J^{\prime\prime}\to J^{\prime}
such that
s_{1}s=s_{2}s.

Definition 1.2.6

(Nice subcategory of shrinkings)
.

A subcategory
\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathsf{Shr}
is called a nice subcategory of shrinkings, if the following properties are satisfied:

(1)

for any interval
J
, there is an interval
J^{\prime}\in\mathbf{S}
such that there exists a shrinking
J^{\prime}\to J;

(2)

for any
J,J^{\prime}\in\mathbf{S}
such that there is a shrinking
J^{\prime}\to J,
there is a morphism
J^{\prime}\to J
in
\mathbf{S}
.

Definition 1.2.7

(Cofinal functors)
.

A functor
F:D\to C
is called left cofinal if for any
c\in C
, the relative over-category
F_{/c}
is (not empty and) connected. It is well known that precomposition with left cofinal functors do not change limits. Dually, a functor
F:D\to C
is called right cofinal, if for any
c\in C
, the relative under-category
F_{c/}
is (not empty and) connected. Precomposition with right cofinal functors preserves colimits.

Proposition 1.2.8
.

If
\mathbf{S}
is a nice subcategory of shrinkings, the functor

(1.2.4)

\mathbf{S}\to\mathsf{HShr}

is left cofinal.

Proof.

Let us denote the functor by
F:\mathbf{S}\to\mathsf{HShr}
. Then we need to show that the relative slice category
F_{/J}
is connected, for any
J\in\mathsf{HShr}
. Property (1) implies that
F_{/J}
is not empty. Take two objects
\alpha_{1}:F(J_{1})\to J
and
\alpha_{2}:F(J_{2})\to J
of
F_{/J}
. Consider an object
J_{3}
of
\mathbf{S}
such that there is a shrinking
J_{3}\to J_{1}\vee J_{2}
. Since we have shrinkings
J_{1}\vee J_{2}\to J_{1}
and
J_{1}\vee J_{2}\to J_{2}
, this defines an object
\alpha_{3}:F(J_{3})\to J
in
F_{/J}
with two morphisms
\alpha_{1}\leftarrow\alpha_{3}\to\alpha_{2}
in
F_{/J}
. Therefore
F_{/J}
is contractible.
∎

Definition 1.2.9

(Standard intervals)
.

We denote by
I_{n},

n\geq 0,
the interval with the set of vertices
\{0,1,\dots,n\}
and arrows
2i\to 2i+1
and
2j+2\to 2j+1
for all possible
i,j
. We also consider the opposite interval
I^{\mathrm{op}}_{n}
.

(1.2.5)

I_{4}:\ 0\to 1\leftarrow 2\to 3\leftarrow 4,\hskip 28.45274ptI_{4}^{\mathrm{op}}:\ 0\leftarrow 1\to 2\leftarrow 3\to 4.

The digraphs
I_{n},I_{n}^{\mathrm{op}},n\geq 0
are called standard intervals. We will also use notations

(1.2.6)

I_{n}^{+1}=I_{n},\hskip 28.45274ptI_{n}^{-1}=I_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}.

Definition 1.2.10

(Left, right and central truncations)
.

The standard left and right truncations are defined as maps between standard intervals

(1.2.7)

l:I_{n+1}^{-\varepsilon}\to I_{n}^{\varepsilon},\hskip 28.45274ptr:I_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}\to I_{n}^{\varepsilon},

where
n\geq 1,

\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\},

defined by the formulas
l(0)=0
and
l(k)=k-1
for
1\leq k\leq n+1;
and
r(k)=k
for
0\leq k\leq n
and
r(n+1)=n
(see
[
CK24
, p.2861]
). We also consider central truncations
c=lr
and their squares

(1.2.8)

c:I_{n+2}^{-\varepsilon}\to I^{\varepsilon}_{n},\hskip 28.45274ptc^{2}:I_{n+4}\to I_{n}.

Example 1.2.11

(Examples of nice subcategories of shrinkings)
.

(1)

\mathsf{Shr}
is a nice subcategory of shrinkings.

(2)

Full subcategory of
\mathsf{Shr}
spanned by standard intervals is a nice subcategory of shrinkings.

(3)

The subcategory defined by the sequence

\dots\xrightarrow{l}I^{\mathrm{op}}_{4}\xrightarrow{r}I^{\mathrm{op}}_{3}\xrightarrow{l}I_{2}\xrightarrow{r}I_{1}

is a nice subcategory of shrinkings. Here we mean that all objects of this category are objects of this sequence and morphisms are compositions of morphisms from this sequence. This category is denoted by

{\sf St}\subseteq\mathsf{Shr}

and called the standard interval category.

(4)

The subcategory defined by the sequence

\dots\xrightarrow{r}I_{4}\xrightarrow{r}I_{3}\xrightarrow{r}I_{2}\xrightarrow{r}I_{1}

is a nice subcategory of shrinkings.

(5)

The subcategory defined by the sequence

\dots\xrightarrow{l}I_{4}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{l}I_{3}\xrightarrow{l}I_{2}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{l}I_{1}

is a nice subcategory of shrinkings.

(6)

The subcategory, whose objects are the intervals
I_{n}
, and morphisms are the maps
I_{nm}\to I_{n}
defined by
x\mapsto[x/m]
, where
m
is odd, is a nice subcategory of shrinkings.

Example 1.2.12

(Cantor intervals)
.

Here we give another example of a nice subcategory of shrinkings.
A Cantor interval
I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{n},n\geq 1
is an interval whose set of vertices is
\{0,\dots,2^{n}-1\}
. Arrows of
I^{\sf Cant}_{n}
are defined as follows. Here we will use the binary number system
[a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}]=\sum 2^{n-i-1}a_{i},

a_{i}\in\{0,1\}.
Then there is an arrow

[a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1},0,1,\dots,1]\to[a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1},1,0,\dots,0]

for even
k
, and an arrow

[a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1},1,0,\dots,0]\to[a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1},0,1,\dots,1]

for odd
k.
For example:

I_{1}^{\mathsf{Cant}}:[0]\to[1],

I_{2}^{\mathsf{Cant}}:[00]\leftarrow[01]\to[10]\leftarrow[11],

I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{3}:[000]\to[001]\leftarrow[010]\to[011]\to[100]\to[101]\leftarrow[110]\to[111].

For
m\geq n
we define the projection map

{\sf pr}:I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{m}\longrightarrow I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{n}

by the formula
{\sf pr}([a_{0},\dots,a_{m-1}])=[a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}].
The subcategory defined by the sequence of Cantor intervals

\dots\xrightarrow{\sf pr}I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{4}\xrightarrow{\sf pr}I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{3}\xrightarrow{\sf pr}I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{2}\xrightarrow{\sf pr}I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{1}

is a nice category of shrinkings.

1.3.
Cubical homotopy groups

In this subsection we show that several different definitions of cubical homotopy groups
A_{*}(G)
of digraphs are equivalent.

Definition 1.3.1

(Cubical homotopy groups)
.

For a pointed digraph
G=(G,g)
and
n\geq 0
, we set

(1.3.1)

A_{n}(G)=\underset{(J_{1},\dots,J_{n})\in(\mathsf{Shr}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}(J_{i},\partial J_{i}),G\right],

where the colimit is taken over
(\mathsf{Shr}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}
, the product of
n
copies of the opposite category of shrinkings.

Remark 1.3.2
.

From the definition it is clear that if
G=(G,g)
and
G^{\prime}=(G^{\prime},g^{\prime})
are pointed digraphs, then any pointed homotopy equivalence
\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism
A_{*}(G)\cong A_{*}(G^{\prime})
.

Remark 1.3.3

(The structure of a group)
.

In this section we don’t use the structure of a group on
A_{n}(G).
For now, it suffices to define it as a set which depends naturally of a pointed digraph. The reader who wants to know an elementary definition of the structure of a group can find it in
[
LWYZ24
, Prop.4.7]
for digraphs and in
[
BKLW01
, Prop.3.5]
for graphs. Further we will show that
A_{n}(G)\cong\pi_{n}(N_{\infty}G),
where
N_{\infty}G
is a cubical Kan complex (Proposition
2.5.3
). This statement automatically determines the structure of the group on
A_{n}(G).

Proposition 1.3.4
.

Let
\mathbf{S}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{n}
be
n
nice subcategories of shrinkings. Then, for any pointed digraph
G
and
n\geq 0
, the map

(1.3.2)

\underset{(J_{1},\dots,J_{n})\in\mathbf{S}_{1}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\dots\times\mathbf{S}_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}(J_{i},\partial J_{i}),G\right]\longrightarrow A_{n}(G)

is a bijection.

Proof.

By the definition
A_{n}(G)
is defined as a colimit of a functor
F:(\mathsf{Shr}^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
, where
F(J_{1},\dots,J_{n})=\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}(J_{i},\partial J_{i}),G\right]
. It is easy to see that this functor factors via a functor from a homotopy category
\tilde{F}:(\mathsf{HShr}^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
. By Proposition
1.2.8
, the functors
(\mathsf{Shr}^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\to(\mathsf{HShr}^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\mathbf{S}_{1}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\dots\times\mathbf{S}_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}\to(\mathsf{HShr}^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
are right cofinal. Then the result follows from the fact that a precomposition with right cofinal functors preserves colimits.
∎

Proposition 1.3.5
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a nice subcategory of shrinkings. Then, for any pointed digraph
G
and
n\geq 0
, the map

(1.3.3)

\underset{J\in\mathbf{S}^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>\left[(J,\partial J)^{\otimes n},G\right]\longrightarrow A_{n}(G)

is a bijection.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{HShr}
is cofiltered (Proposition
1.2.4
), the functor
\mathsf{HShr}\to\mathsf{HShr}^{n}
is left cofinal. By Proposition
1.2.8
, the functor
\mathbf{S}\to\mathsf{HShr}
is left cofinal. Therefore
\mathbf{S}^{\mathrm{op}}\to(\mathsf{HShr}^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
is right cofinal. The rest of the proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
1.3.4
.
∎

Corollary 1.3.6
.

For a pointed digraph
G
and
n\geq 0
, there is a natural bijection

(1.3.4)

A_{n}(G)\cong\underset{J\in\mathsf{Shr}^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}[(J,\partial J)^{\otimes n},G].

Corollary 1.3.7
.

For any pointed digraph
G
and
n\geq 0
, the cubical homotopy group
A_{n}(G)
is isomorphic to the colimit of each of the following sequences.

(1.3.5)

\displaystyle[(I_{1},\partial I_{1})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{r^{*}}[(I_{2},\partial I_{2})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{l^{*}}[(I_{3}^{\mathrm{op}},\partial I_{3}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{r^{*}}\dots

(1.3.6)

\displaystyle[(I_{1},\partial I_{1})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{r^{*}}[(I_{2},\partial I_{2})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{r^{*}}[(I_{3},\partial I_{3})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{r^{*}}\dots

(1.3.7)

\displaystyle[(I_{1},\partial I_{1})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{l^{*}}[(I_{2}^{\mathrm{op}},\partial I_{2}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{l^{*}}[(I_{3},\partial I_{3})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{l^{*}}\dots

(1.3.8)

\displaystyle[(I_{4},\partial I_{4})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}[(I_{8},\partial I_{8})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}[(I_{16},\partial I_{16})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}\dots

(1.3.9)

\displaystyle[(I_{1}^{\mathsf{Cant}},\partial I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{1})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{{\sf pr}^{*}}[(I_{2}^{\mathsf{Cant}},\partial I^{\mathsf{Cant}}_{2})^{\otimes n},G]\xrightarrow{{\sf pr}^{*}}\dots

Definition 1.3.8

(Digraph spheres)
.

For any interval
J
and
n\geq 0
, we denote by
S^{n}_{J}
the quotient of
J^{\otimes n}
by
\partial J^{\otimes n}
.

(1.3.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial J^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{n}_{J}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

If
J=I_{m}
, we denote it by
S^{n}_{m}.

Corollary 1.3.9
.

For a nice category of shrinkings
\mathbf{S}
, a pointed digraph
G
and
n\geq 0
, there is a natural bijection

(1.3.11)

A_{n}(G)\cong\underset{J\in\mathbf{S}^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>[S^{n}_{J},G]_{*}.

1.4.
Path and loop digraphs

Both Carranza–Kapulkin
[
CK24
]
and Li–Wu–Yau–Zhang
[
LWYZ24
]
introduce notions of a path digraph and a loop digraph. While their definitions satisfy similar properties, they are not equivalent, even when restricted to the case of ordinary graphs. In this subsection, we present a unified approach to the path digraph construction. We define a path digraph, denoted by
P_{\mathbf{S}}G
, which depends on a nice subcategory of shrinkings
\mathbf{S}
(Definition
1.2.6
). By varying the choice of
\mathbf{S}
, one recovers the specific versions developed in both
[
CK24
]
and
[
LWYZ24
]
. However, while our definition subsumes both previous constructions, the subsequent theory we develop generalizes only that of Carranza–Kapulkin, as our results rely on the assumption that
\mathbf{S}
is cofiltered.

Definition 1.4.1

(Path and loop digraphs)
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a nice subcategory of shrinkings (Definition
1.2.6
) and
G
be a digraph. Then the path digraph
P_{\mathbf{S}}G
is defined by the formula

(1.4.1)

P_{\mathbf{S}}G=\underset{J\in\mathbf{S}^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(J,G).

This construction defines a functor

P_{\mathbf{S}}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{DGra}.

The inclusions of the end-points
j_{0},j_{1}:*\to J
define two maps
p_{0},p_{1}:P_{\mathbf{S}}G\to G
.

If
G=(G,g)
is a pointed digraph, the loop digraph
\Omega_{\mathbf{S}}G
is defined by the formula

(1.4.2)

\Omega_{\mathbf{S}}G=\underset{J\in\mathbf{S}^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}_{*}(S^{1}_{J},G).

This construction defines a functor

\Omega_{\mathbf{S}}:\mathsf{DGra}_{*}\to\mathsf{DGra}_{*}.

Remark 1.4.2
.

The path digraph considered by
Li–Wu–Yau–Zhang in
[
LWYZ24
]
is the path digraph
P_{\mathbf{S}}G
, where
\mathbf{S}=\mathsf{Shr}
is the whole category of shrinkings. The path digraph considered by Carranza–Kapulkin in
[
CK24
]
is the path digraph
P_{\mathbf{S}}G
, where
\mathbf{S}={\sf St}
is the standard interval category (see Example
1.2.11
).
We find the choice
\mathbf{S}={\sf St}
more natural, as
{\sf St}
is cofiltered whereas
\mathsf{Shr}
is not. However there are many other cofiltered nice subcategories of shrinkings that can be useful. Therefore we develop this theory for arbitrary cofiltered nice subcategory of shrinkings
\mathbf{S}
.

Lemma 1.4.3
.

For a cofiltered nice subcategory of shrinkings

\mathbf{S}
, the commutative square

(1.4.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\mathbf{S}}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\mathbf{S}}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{0},p_{1})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,g)}

is a pullback.

Proof.

For any
J
, the square

(1.4.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{*}^{\otimes}(S^{1}_{J},G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(J,G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{0},p_{1})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,g)}

is a pullback. Note that the forgetful functor
\mathsf{DGra}_{*}\to\mathsf{DGra}
creates limits and colimits. Then the statement follows from the fact that, in
\mathsf{DGra}
, filtered colimits commute with finite limits (Proposition
1.1.6
).
∎

Proposition 1.4.4
.

For a cofiltered nice subcategory of shrinkings

\mathbf{S}
, the functors
\Omega_{\mathbf{S}}
and
P_{\mathbf{S}}
preserve finite limits.

Proof.

It follows from the fact that, in
\mathsf{DGra}
and
\mathsf{DGra}_{*}
, filtered colimits commute with finite limits (Proposition
1.1.6
).
∎

Proposition 1.4.5
.

For a cofiltered nice subcategory of shrinkings

\mathbf{S}
, any pointed digraph
G
and any
n\geq 0
, there is a natural isomorphism

(1.4.5)

A_{n+1}(G)\cong A_{n}(\Omega_{\mathbf{S}}G).

Proof.

By Proposition
1.1.25
, there is a natural isomorphism between the set

(1.4.6)

[(J_{1},\partial J_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes(J_{n},\partial J_{n})\otimes(J_{n+1},\partial J_{n+1}),G]

and the set

(1.4.7)

[(J_{1},\partial J_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes(J_{n},\partial J_{n}),\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}^{\otimes}((J_{n+1},\partial J_{n+1}),G)]

Note that

(1.4.8)

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}^{\otimes}((J_{n+1},\partial J_{n+1}),G)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{*}^{\otimes}(S^{1}_{J_{n+1}},G).

Then the statement follows from the fact that
(J_{1},\partial J_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes(J_{n},\partial J_{n})
is a compact object of
\mathsf{PairDGra}/\sim_{\sf rel}
(Proposition
1.1.29
) and Proposition
1.3.4
.
∎

Corollary 1.4.6
.

For a cofiltered nice subcategory of shrinkings

\mathbf{S}
, any pointed digraph
G
and any
n\geq 0
, there is a natural isomorphism

(1.4.9)

A_{n}(G)\cong\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}_{\mathbf{S}}G).

2.
Cubical nerves of digraphs

In this section, we study the “cubical nerve functors” from the category of digraphs to the category of cubical sets,
N_{m}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
, and their colimit
N_{\infty}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
. We show that for any digraph
G
, the cubical set
N_{\infty}G
is a fibrant replacement of
N_{1}G
, and that
A_{*}(G)\cong\pi_{*}(N_{\infty}G)\cong\pi_{*}(|N_{1}G|).
These results generalize results of
[
CK24
]
. We also introduce the
\infty
-category of digraphs
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
and show that the functor
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
induced by
N_{1}
is isomorphic to the mapping space from the point
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}(*,-)
.

2.1.
Cubical sets

In this subsection we collect some information about cubical sets that we use further. A more detailed information about cubical sets with connections can be found in
[
Cis14
,
CK23
,
CK24
]
.

Definition 2.1.1

((Co)cubical objects)
.

By the box category
\Box
we mean the box category with (both positive and negative) connections. It is the subcategory of the category of posets whose objects are
[1]^{n},n\geq 0
and morphisms are compositions of the maps

(2.1.1)

\displaystyle\partial^{i,\varepsilon}:[1]^{n}\to[1]^{n-1},\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n,\ \varepsilon\in\{0,1\}

(2.1.2)

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}:[1]^{n}\to[1]^{n-1},\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}:[1]^{n}\to[1]^{n-1},\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n-1,\ \varepsilon\in\{0,1\}

called coface maps, codegeneracy maps and coconnections respectively, defined by the formulas

(2.1.4)

\displaystyle\partial^{i,\varepsilon}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},\varepsilon,x_{i},\dots,x_{n}),

(2.1.5)

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n}),

(2.1.6)

\displaystyle\gamma^{i,0}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},\max(x_{i},x_{i+1}),x_{i+2},\dots,x_{n}),

(2.1.7)

\displaystyle\gamma^{i,1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},\min(x_{i},x_{i+1}),x_{i+2},\dots,x_{n}).

For a category
C
, the category of cubical objects of
C
is the category of functors

(2.1.8)

{\sf c}C=\mathrm{Fun}(\Box^{\mathrm{op}},C).

A cubical object is uniquely defined by a sequence of objects
X_{n},n\geq 0
, equipped with maps

(2.1.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{i,\varepsilon}:X_{n}\to X_{n-1},\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n,\ \varepsilon\in\{0,1\}

(2.1.10)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}:X_{n-1}\to X_{n},\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n

(2.1.11)

\displaystyle\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}:X_{n-1}\to X_{n},\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n-1,\ \varepsilon\in\{0,1\}

called face maps, degeneracy maps and connections respectively, satisfying the cubical identities:

(2.1.12)

\partial_{i,\varepsilon}\partial_{j,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\partial_{j,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\partial_{i+1,\varepsilon},\text{ for }j\leq i\hskip 28.45274pt\partial_{i,\varepsilon}\sigma_{j}=\begin{cases}\sigma_{j}\partial_{i-1,\varepsilon},&j<i\\
\mathrm{id},&j=i\\
\sigma_{j-1}\partial_{i,\varepsilon},&j>i\end{cases}

(2.1.13)

\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{j}\text{ for }j\leq i,\hskip 28.45274pt\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}\gamma_{j,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\gamma_{j+1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\gamma_{i,\varepsilon},&j>i\\
\gamma_{i+1,\varepsilon}\gamma_{i,\varepsilon},&i=j,\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}\end{cases}

(2.1.14)

\partial_{i,\varepsilon}\gamma_{j,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\gamma_{j,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\partial_{i-1,\varepsilon},&j<i-1\\
\mathrm{id},&j\in\{i-1,i\},\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}\\
\sigma_{j}\partial_{j,\varepsilon},&j\in\{i-1,i\},\varepsilon\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}\\
\gamma_{j-1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\partial_{i,\varepsilon},&j>i\end{cases}\hskip 14.22636pt\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}\sigma_{j}=\begin{cases}\sigma_{j}\gamma_{i-1,\varepsilon},&j<i\\
\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i},&j=i\\
\sigma_{i,\varepsilon}\gamma_{i,\varepsilon},&j>i.\end{cases}

Cocubical objects of
C
are cubical objects of
C^{\mathrm{op}}
. Cubical sets are cubical objects in the category of sets

(2.1.15)

\mathsf{cSet}=\mathrm{Fun}(\Box^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{Set}).

In other words, cubical sets are presheaves over the box category. For a cubical set
X
, elements of
X_{n}=X([1]^{n})
are called
n
-cubes of
X
. An
n
-cube
x
of
X
is called non-degenerate if it can not be presented as
x=\sigma_{i}(y)
or
x=\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}(y)
for an
(n+1)
-cube
y.

Notation 2.1.2

(Standard cube, its boundary, horn and sphere)
.

We use the standard notations for the standard examples of cubical sets. The standard cubes, their boundaries, horns and spheres are denoted as follows:

(2.1.16)

\displaystyle\Box^{n}=\Box(-,[1]^{n}),\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle\partial\Box^{n}=\bigcup_{(i,\varepsilon)}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}(\Box^{n-1}),

(2.1.17)

\displaystyle\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}=\bigcup_{(j,\delta)\neq(i,\varepsilon)}\partial^{j,\delta}(\Box^{n-1}),\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle S^{n}=\Box^{n}/\partial\Box^{n}.

Theorem 2.1.3

(
[
DKLS24
, Th.1.34, Prop.1.35]
,
[
Cis14
, Th.1.7]
)
.

There is a proper cofibrantly generated model category structure on
\mathsf{cSet}
such that

(2.1.18)

\displaystyle\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}\hookrightarrow\Box^{n},\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n,\ \varepsilon\in\{0,1\}

is a set of generating acyclic cofibrations,

(2.1.19)

\displaystyle\partial\Box^{n}\hookrightarrow\Box^{n},\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle n\geq 0

is a set of generating cofibrations, and cofibrations are monomorphisms. Moreover, weak equivalences are closed under finite products.

Definition 2.1.4

(Kan fibrations, Kan complexes, anodyne maps)
.

In this model category structure, fibrations are referred to as Kan fibrations, fibrant objects as Kan complexes, and acyclic cofibrations as anodyne maps.

Definition 2.1.5

(Pointed cubical sets)
.

For any model category
M
, one can consider the model category of pointed objects
M_{*}
, whose cofibrations, fibrations and weak equivalences are those morphisms of pointed objects whose underlying morphism in
M
is a cofibration, fibration and weak equivalence respectively
[
Hov07
, Prop.1.1.8]
. We consider the model category of pointed cubical sets
\mathsf{cSet}_{*}
.

Definition 2.1.6

(Geometric realization and singular cubical set)
.

There is an obvious cocubical topological space defined by the topological cubes
[0,1]^{n},
where the connections are defined using the functions
\max,\min:[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1]
. By the universal property of the Yoneda embedding
[
GZ12
, Prop.II.1.3]
, the cocubical object induces an adjunction

(2.1.20)

|\cdot|:\mathsf{cSet}\leftrightarrows{\sf Top}:{\sf Sing}_{\Box},

where
|\cdot|
is a colimit-preserving functor such that

|\Box^{n}|\cong[0,1]^{n},

and
{\sf Sing}_{\Box}(T)_{n}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\sf Top}([0,1]^{n},T).

Theorem 2.1.7

(
[
CK23
, Cor.2.25]
)
.

The adjunction (
2.1.20
) is a Quillen equivalence, where
{\sf Top}
is treated as a model category with the classical Quillen model structure.

Corollary 2.1.8
.

A map of cubical sets
f
is a weak equivalence if and only if its geometric realization
|f|
is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Since
(|\cdot|,\mathsf{Sing}_{\Box})
is a Quillen equivalence and all objects of
\mathsf{cSet}
are cofibrant, it follows from
[
Hov07
, Cor.1.3.16, Lem.1.1.12]
.
∎

Corollary 2.1.9
.

Weak equivalences of cubical sets are closed under (possibly infinite) coproducts.

Corollary 2.1.10
.

For any topological space
T
,
\mathsf{Sing}_{\Box}(T)
is a Kan complex.

Definition 2.1.11

(Geometric product of cubical sets)
.

Consider the functor
\otimes:\Box\times\Box\to\Box
defined by
[1]^{n}\otimes[1]^{m}=[1]^{n+m}
. Then the geometric tensor product of cubical sets is defined as the left Kan extension

(2.1.21)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Box\times\Box}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Box}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cSet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cSet}\times\mathsf{cSet}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}

This construction defines a monoidal category structure on
\mathsf{cSet}
with
\Box^{0}
as the monoidal unit. Moreover, the geometric realization sends the geometric product of cubical sets to product of topological spaces

(2.1.22)

|X\otimes Y|\cong|X|\times|Y|.

The geometric product is not symmetric in general
X\otimes Y\not\cong Y\otimes X.
However, it is biclosed. For a cubical set
X
, we denote by

(2.1.23)

\mathrm{Hom}_{L}(X,-),\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(X,-)

the right adjoint functors to
-\otimes X
and
X\otimes-
respectively.

Proposition 2.1.12

(Cylinder)
.

For any cubical set
X
, the cubical set
X\otimes\Box^{1}
equipped with the maps

(2.1.24)

X\sqcup X\xrightarrow{\ (\partial^{0},\partial^{1})\ }X\otimes\Box^{1}\xrightarrow{\ \sf pr\ }X

is a cylinder object with respect to the model category structure on
\mathsf{cSet}
. Similarly, for any pointed cubical set
(X,x_{0})
the object
(X\otimes\Box^{1})/(\{x_{0}\}\otimes\Box^{1})
equipped with the induced morphisms

(2.1.25)

X\vee X\longrightarrow(X\otimes\Box^{1})/(\{x_{0}\}\otimes\Box^{1})\longrightarrow X

is a cylinder object in
\mathsf{cSet}_{*}.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
X\sqcup X\to X\otimes\Box^{1}
is a monomorphism.
Since
|X|\times[0,1]\to|X|
is a weak equivalence,
X\otimes\Box^{1}\to X
is also a weak equivalence (Corollary
2.1.8
). It follows that
X\otimes\Box^{1}
is a cylinder object in
\mathsf{cSet}
. The proof of the pointed version is similar.
∎

Definition 2.1.13

(Homotopy for cubical sets)
.

Let
A
and
X
be cubical sets. Homotopy equivalence
\sim
on
\mathsf{cSet}(A,X)
is denoted as the left homotopy with respect to the functorial cylinder object
A\otimes\Box^{1}
. The quotient set is denoted by

(2.1.26)

[A,X]=\mathsf{cSet}(A,X)/\sim.

If
X
is a Kan complex,
[A,X]
is isomorphic to the hom-set in the homotopy category

[A,X]\cong{\sf Ho}(\mathsf{cSet})(A,X).

A map of cubical sets
f:X\to Y
is called homotopy equivalence, if there is a map
g:Y\to X
such that
fg\sim\mathrm{id}
and
gf\sim\mathrm{id}
.

For the pointed version, the pointed homotopy is defined using the cylinder object
(A\otimes\Box^{1})/(\{a_{0}\}\otimes\Box^{1})
. The quotient set in this case is denoted by

(2.1.27)

[A,X]_{*}=\mathsf{cSet}_{*}(A,X)/\sim.

If
X
is a pointed Kan complex, we have

[A,X]_{*}\cong{\sf Ho}(\mathsf{cSet}_{*})(A,X).

Proposition 2.1.14
.

A homotopy equivalence of cubical sets is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

If
f
is a homotopy equivalence of cubical sets, then
|f|
is a homotopy equivalence of topological spaces. Then the statement follows from Corollary
2.1.8
.
∎

Definition 2.1.15

(Homotopy group of a Kan complex)
.

For a pointed Kan complex
X\in\mathsf{cSet}_{*}
, the
n
-th homotopy group is defined by

(2.1.28)

\pi_{n}(X)=[S^{n},X]_{*}.

Theorem 2.1.16

(
[
CK23
, Th.3.24, 3.25]
)
.

For any pointed Kan complex
X
, and any pointed topological space
T
, there are natural isomorphisms

(2.1.29)

\pi_{*}(X)\cong\pi_{*}(|X|),\hskip 28.45274pt\pi_{*}(T)\cong\pi_{*}(\mathsf{Sing}_{\Box}(T)).

Corollary 2.1.17
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of Kan complexes. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

f
is a weak equivalence.

(2)

f
is a homotopy equivalence.

(3)

for any
x_{0}\in X_{0}
, the map
f_{*}:\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\to\pi_{n}(Y,f(x_{0}))
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
2.1.16
, Corollary
2.1.8
and the fact that, for any model category, strong equivalences between fibrant-cofibrant objects coincide with weak equivalences
[
Hov07
, Cor.1.2.9]
.
∎

2.2.
Cubical nerves

Definition 2.2.1

(
J
-nerve and
J
-realization)
.

Let
J\in\mathsf{DGra}
be an interval with the set of vertices
\{0,\dots,m\}
(Definition
1.1.26
). It is easy to check that the maximum and minimum functions define maps of digraphs

(2.2.1)

\max:J\otimes J\to J,\hskip 28.45274pt\min:J\otimes J\to J.

These maps allow to define the cocubical digraph

(2.2.2)

\Box\longrightarrow\mathsf{DGra},\hskip 28.45274pt[1]^{n}\mapsto J^{\otimes n}

where the coface maps, codegeneracy maps and coconnections are defined by the formulas

(2.2.3)

\displaystyle\partial^{i,\varepsilon}(j_{1},\dots,j_{n})

\displaystyle=(j_{1},\dots,j_{i-1},\varepsilon m,j_{i},\dots,j_{n}),\hskip 28.45274pt\varepsilon\in\{0,1\},

(2.2.4)

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}(j_{1},\dots,j_{n})

\displaystyle=(j_{1},\dots,j_{i-1},j_{i+1},\dots,j_{n}),

(2.2.5)

\displaystyle\gamma^{i,0}(j_{1},\dots,j_{n})

\displaystyle=(j_{1},\dots,j_{i-1},\max(j_{i},j_{i+1}),j_{i+2},\dots,j_{n}),

(2.2.6)

\displaystyle\gamma^{i,1}(j_{1},\dots,j_{n})

\displaystyle=(j_{1},\dots,j_{i-1},\min(j_{i},j_{i+1}),j_{i+2},\dots,j_{n}).

By the universal property of the Yoneda embedding, we obtain an adjunction

(2.2.7)

|\cdot|_{J}:\mathsf{cSet}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{DGra}:N_{J},

where
|\cdot|_{J}
is the colimit-preserving functor such that
|\Box^{n}|_{J}=J^{\otimes n}
and
(N_{J}G)_{n}=\mathrm{Hom}(J^{\otimes n},G)
.
The functor
|\cdot|_{J}
is called
J
-realization, and the functor
N_{J}
is called cubical
J
-nerve. We also use the notations

(2.2.8)

|\cdot|^{\varepsilon}_{n}=|\cdot|_{I_{n}^{\varepsilon}},\hskip 28.45274ptN_{n}^{\varepsilon}=N_{I_{n}^{\varepsilon}},

where
\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\},

and

(2.2.9)

|\cdot|_{n}=|\cdot|_{n}^{+1},\hskip 14.22636pt|\cdot|_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}=|\cdot|^{-1}_{n},\hskip 14.22636ptN_{n}=N^{+1}_{n},\hskip 14.22636ptN^{\mathrm{op}}_{n}=N^{-1}_{n}.

Proposition 2.2.2
.

For cubical sets
X
and
Y
, and an interval
J
, there is a natural isomorphism

(2.2.10)

|X\otimes Y|_{J}\cong|X|_{J}\otimes|Y|_{J}.

Proof.

The functors
|-\otimes-|_{J}
and
|-|_{J}\otimes|-|_{J}
are the left Kan extension of their restrictions
\Box\times\Box\to\mathsf{DGra}
. Therefore, it is sufficient to prove the isomorphism for the restrictions. It follows from the isomorphism
J^{\otimes n}\otimes J^{\otimes m}\cong J^{\otimes n+m}
(which is natural in
n
and
m
).
∎

Proposition 2.2.3
.

Let
X
be a cubical set,
G
be a digraph and
J
be an interval. Then there are isomorphisms

(2.2.11)

\mathrm{Hom}_{L}(X,N_{J}G)\cong N_{J}(\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(|X|_{J},G))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(X,N_{J}G)

natural in
X
and
G
.

Proof.

Since the square

(2.2.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cSet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cSet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DGra}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DGra}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes X}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|-|_{J}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|-|_{J}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes|X|_{J}}

commutes up to natural isomorphism by Proposition
2.2.2
, the corresponding square of adjoint functors

(2.2.13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DGra}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DGra}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cSet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cSet}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(|X|_{J},-)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{J}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{J}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{L}(X,-)}

also commutes up to a natural isomorphism. In order to show that the isomorphism is natural in
X
, we use the equivalence between left adjoint and right adjoint functors, which follows from the universal property of the Yoneda embedding

(2.2.14)

\mathrm{Fun}^{L}(\mathsf{cSet},\mathsf{DGra})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\Box,\mathsf{DGra})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{R}(\mathsf{DGra},\mathsf{cSet}).

The proof of the second isomorphism is similar.
∎

Definition 2.2.4

(The infinite cubical nerve)
.

The infinite cubical nerve
NG
is defined as the colimit

(2.2.15)

N_{\infty}G=\underset{J\in{\sf St}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>N_{J}G,

where
J
runs over the standard interval category (Example
1.2.11
(
3
)).
In other words,
NG
is the colimit of the sequence

(2.2.16)

N_{1}G\xrightarrow{r^{*}}N_{2}G\xrightarrow{l^{*}}N_{3}^{\mathrm{op}}G\xrightarrow{r^{*}}N_{4}^{\mathrm{op}}G\xrightarrow{l^{*}}\dots

It is easy to check that it is also equal to the colimit of the following sequences:

(2.2.17)

\displaystyle N_{2}G\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}N_{6}G\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}N_{10}G\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}N_{14}G\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}\dots

(2.2.18)

\displaystyle N_{2}G\xrightarrow{(l^{2})^{*}}N_{4}G\xrightarrow{(r^{2})^{*}}N_{6}G\xrightarrow{(l^{2})^{*}}N_{8}G\xrightarrow{(r^{2})^{*}}\dots

(2.2.19)

\displaystyle N_{4}G\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}N_{8}G\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}N_{12}G\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}N_{16}G\xrightarrow{(c^{2})^{*}}\dots

Proposition 2.2.5
.

The functor

N_{\infty}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
preserves finite limits.

Proof.

It follows from the fact that
N_{m}
preserves finite limits, for any
m
, and that filtered colimits in
\mathsf{cSet}
commute with finite limits.
∎

2.3.
N_{\infty}G
is a Kan complex

In this subsection we prove a generalization of
[
CK24
, Th.4.1(1)]
from the the case of graphs to the case of digraphs.

Theorem 2.3.1
.

For any digraph
G
,
N_{\infty}G
is a Kan complex.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

For any integers
m\geq 0,n\geq 1
and any
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
,
there is a digraph map

(2.3.1)

\Phi:|\Box^{n}|_{6m}\to|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{2m}

such that the following diagram commutes.

(2.3.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{6m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{2m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\Box^{n}|_{6m}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{2m}}
\hookleftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

Proof.

If
n=1
, it is clear. Assume that
n\geq 2
. Consider the map
\phi:I_{6m}\to I_{6m}
given by

(2.3.3)

\phi(x)=\begin{cases}2m,&x\leq 2m,\\
x,&2m\leq x\leq 4m,\\
4m,&4m\leq x,\end{cases}

and the map
D:I^{\otimes n}_{6m}\to I_{2m}
defined by

(2.3.4)

D(v)=\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq n}\{|v_{j}-\phi(v_{j})|\}.

Note that

c^{2m}:I_{6m}\to I_{2m}

is given by

(2.3.5)

c^{2m}(x)=\begin{cases}0,&x\leq 2m,\\
x-2m,&2m\leq x\leq 4m,\\
2m,&x\geq 4m.\end{cases}

Now we define a map
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}:I^{\otimes n}_{6m}\to I^{\otimes n}_{2m}
by the formula

(2.3.6)

\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{k}=\begin{cases}c^{2m}(v_{k}),&k\neq i,\\
\max\{c^{2m}(v_{i}),2m-D(\sigma_{i}v)\},&i=k,\varepsilon=0,\\
\min\{c^{2m}(v_{i}),D(\sigma_{i}v)\},&i=k,\varepsilon=1.\end{cases}

Let us check that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}
is a digraph map. Take a non-degenerate arrow
v\to v^{\prime}
in
I_{6m}^{\otimes n}
. Then there is an index
k
such that there is a non-degenerate arrow
v_{k}\to v_{k}^{\prime}
in
I_{6m}
and
v_{j}=v_{j}^{\prime}
, for all
j\neq k
. By the definition of
I_{6m}
,
v_{k}
is even and
v_{k}^{\prime}
is odd such that
|v_{k}-v_{k^{\prime}}|=1
. We need to prove that there is an arrow
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)\to\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v^{\prime})
in
I_{2m}^{\otimes n}
. Consider three cases.

(1)

Assume that
k\neq i
and
c^{2m}(v_{k})=c^{2m}(v_{k}^{\prime})
. Since
v_{j}=v^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\neq k
and
c^{2m}(v_{k})=c^{2m}(v_{k}^{\prime})
, we obtain
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{s}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v^{\prime})_{s}
for
s\neq i.
Therefore we need to check that there is an arrow
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{i}\to\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v^{\prime})_{i}.
The equation
c^{2m}(v_{k})=c^{2m}(v_{k}^{\prime})
implies that either
0\leq v_{k},v_{k}^{\prime}\leq 2m
or
4m\leq v_{k},v^{\prime}_{k}\leq 6m
.
Using that
v_{k}
is even and
v_{k}^{\prime}
is odd, we obtain that there are arrows
D(\sigma_{i}v)\to D(\sigma_{i}v^{\prime})
and
2m-D(\sigma_{i}v)\to 2m-D(\sigma_{i}v)
(note that we always have an arrow from a vertex to itself). It follows that we have an arrow
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{i}\to\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v^{\prime})_{i}
;

(2)

Assume that
k\neq i
and there is a non-degenerate arrow
c^{2m}(v_{k})\to c^{2m}(v_{k}^{\prime})
. Then
2m\leq v_{k},v_{k}^{\prime}\leq 4m
, and
D(\sigma_{i}v)=D(\sigma_{i}v_{i}^{\prime})
. It follows that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{j}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{j}
for
j\neq k,
and there is an arrow
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{k}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{k}
. Hence, we have an arrow
\Phi(v)\to\Phi(v^{\prime})
;

(3)

Assume that
k=i
. Then
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{j}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{j}
for
j\neq i.
Since
v_{j}=v_{j}^{\prime}
for
j\neq i
, we have
D(\sigma_{i}v)=D(\sigma_{i}v^{\prime})
. Hence, the arrow
c^{2m}(v_{i})\to c^{2m}(v_{i}^{\prime})
induces an arrow
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{i}\to\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v^{\prime})_{i}.

Therefore
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}
is a digraph map.

Let us check that the image of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}
lies in the subdigraph

(2.3.7)

|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{2m}\subseteq I_{2m}^{\otimes n}

consisting of vertices
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
such that either there exists
k\neq i
such that
v_{k}\in\{0,2m\}
, or
v_{i}=(1-\varepsilon)2m.

For any vertex
v=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})\in I_{6m}^{\otimes n}
, we consider two cases.

(1)

Assume that there exists
k\neq i
such that
c^{2m}(v_{k})\in\{0,2m\}
. Then
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{k}\in\{0,2m\}
and we have
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)\in|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{2m};

(2)

Assume that
0<c^{2m}(v_{k})<2m
for all
k\neq i
. Then
2m<v_{k}<4m
for all
k\neq i
, and
D(\sigma_{i}v)=0
. It follows that

\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,0}(v)_{i}=\max\{c^{2m}(v_{i}),2m-D(\sigma_{i}v)\}=2m

and

\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,1}(v)_{i}=\min\{D(\sigma_{i}v),c^{2m}(v_{i})\}=0.

Thus,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{i}=(1-\varepsilon)2m
and
\tilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}(v)\in|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{2m}.

Therefore, the image of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i,\varepsilon}
is in
|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{2m}
and we can consider the induced digraph map

(2.3.8)

\Phi_{i,\varepsilon}:I^{\otimes n}_{6m}\longrightarrow|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{2m}.

Finally, we show that the diagram (
2.3.2
) is commutative. We need to check that for any
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\in|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{6m}\subseteq I^{\otimes n}_{6m}
we have
\Phi_{i,\varepsilon}(v)=c^{2m}(v).
Since
\Phi_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{k}=c^{2m}(v_{k})
for any
k\neq i,
it is sufficient to check that
\Phi_{i,\varepsilon}(v)_{i}=c^{2m}(v_{i}).
Consider the following four cases.

(1)

Assume that
\varepsilon=0
and there is
k\neq i
such that
v_{k}\in\{0,6m\}.
Then
D(\sigma_{i}v)=2m
, and
\max\{c^{2m}(v_{i}),2m-D(\sigma_{i}v)\}=c^{2m}(v_{i})
;

(2)

Assume that
\varepsilon=0
, and
v_{i}=6m
. Then
c^{2m}(v_{i})=2m
, which follows that
\max\{c^{2m}(v_{i}),2m-D(\sigma_{i}v)\}=2m=c^{2m}(v_{i})
;

(3)

Assume that
\varepsilon=1
and there is
k\neq i
such that
v_{k}\in\{0,6m\}
. Then
D(\sigma_{i}v)=2m
, and
\min\{D(\sigma_{i}v),c^{2m}(v_{i})\}=c^{2m}(v_{i})
;

(4)

Assume that
\varepsilon=1
and
v_{i}=0
. Then
c^{2m}(v_{i})=0
, and
\min\{D(\sigma_{i}v),c^{2m}(v_{i})\}=0=c^{2m}(v_{i})
.

In conclusion,
the diagram is commutative.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.3.1
.

Since
\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}
has a finite number of non-degenerate cubes (cubes that can not be presented as degeneracy or connection of some other cube), it is a compact object of
\mathsf{cSet}
, and we have an isomorphism

(2.3.9)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathsf{cSet}}(\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon},N_{\infty}G)\cong\underset{I_{4m}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathsf{cSet}}(\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon},N_{4m}G),

where the maps are induced by
c^{2}:I_{4(m+1)}\to I_{4m}
. Hence for any
f:\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}\to N_{\infty}G
, there is a cubical map
g:\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}\to N_{4m}G
,
for some integer
m\geq 0,
whose composition with
N_{4m}G\to N_{\infty}G
is
f
. We denote the adjoint map by
\bar{g}:|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{4m}\to G
. By Lemma
2.3.2
there is a map
\bar{h}:|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{12m}\to G
such that the diagram

(2.3.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{12m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{4m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\Box^{n}|_{12m}}.}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{4m}}
\hookleftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{h}}

is commutative. It follows that there is a map
h:\Box^{n}\to N_{12m}G
such that the diagram

(2.3.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{4m}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Box^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{12m}G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c^{4m})^{*}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

is commutative. Hence
f
can be extended to a map
\hat{f}:\Box^{n}\to N_{\infty}G
, which is defined as the composition of
h
with
N_{12m}G\to N_{\infty}G
.
∎

2.4.
Equivalence of cubical nerves

In this subsection we generalize
[
CK24
, Th.4.1(2)]
to the case of digraphs.

Theorem 2.4.1
.

For any digraph
G
, the map
N_{1}G\to N_{\infty}G
is anodyne.

Recall that
r:I_{m+2}\to I_{m+1}
is the shrinking map such that
r(m+2)=m+1
and
r(v)=v
for any
v\leq m+1
. Let
r^{k}:I_{m+2}\to I_{m+2-k}
denote the
k
-fold application of
r
. That is,

r^{k}(v)=\begin{cases}v&v\leq m+2-k\\
m+2-k&v>m+2-k.\end{cases}

In particular, we set
r^{0}=\mathrm{id}
.

Notation 2.4.2
.

For
n\geq 1
,
0\leq j\leq n-1
, and
m
an even integer, we define a map

(2.4.1)

\rho_{m+2,j}^{n}:I^{\otimes n}_{m+2}\otimes I_{2}\to I^{\otimes j+1}_{m+2}\otimes I^{\otimes n-j-1}_{m}

as

\rho_{m+2,j}^{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n+1})=\begin{cases}(v_{1},\dots,v_{j+1},r^{2}(v_{j+2}),\dots,r^{2}(v_{n}))&v_{n+1}=0\\
(v_{1},\dots,v_{j},r(v_{j+1}),r^{2}(v_{j+2}),\dots,r^{2}(v_{n}))&v_{n+1}=1\\
(v_{1},\dots,v_{j},r^{2}(v_{j+1}),r^{2}(v_{j+2}),\dots,r^{2}(v_{n}))&v_{n+1}=2.\\
\end{cases}

For simplicity, we often express the above formula in the following way:

(2.4.2)

\rho^{n}_{m+2,j}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n+1})=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j},r^{v_{n+1}}(v_{j+1}),r^{2}(v_{j+2}),\ldots,r^{2}(v_{n})).

The following picture depicts the map
\rho^{1}_{4,0}:I_{4}\otimes I_{2}\to I_{4}
(the picture is read from top to bottom):

(2.4.3)
