The following lemma justifies this definition:

Lemma 2.4.3
.

For
m
even, the map
\rho_{m+2,j}^{n}
is a well defined digraph map.

Proof.

Note that for
\lambda\in\{0,1,2\}
we have

(2.4.4)

\rho_{m+2,j}^{n}|_{I_{m+2}^{\otimes n}\otimes\{\lambda\}}=\mathrm{id}^{j}\otimes r^{\lambda}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j-1}.

Therefore, it is enough to verify this for arrows of
I^{\otimes n}_{m+2}\otimes I_{2}
that differ in the last coordinate. By comparing the formulas of (
2.4.1
), it suffices to prove that we have the following arrows:

(2.4.5)

v_{j+1}\to r(v_{j+1})\leftarrow r^{2}(v_{j+1}).

Indeed, if
v_{j+1}\leq m
, then they are the same; if
v_{j+1}=m+1
, then we have

(2.4.6)

m+1\to m+1\leftarrow m

since
m
is even; if
v_{j+1}=m+2
, then we have

(2.4.7)

m+2\to m+1\leftarrow m

since
m
is even.
∎

Notation 2.4.4
.

For
0\leq j\leq n-1
and even
m
,
define

(2.4.8)

\overline{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}:I^{\otimes n+1}_{m+2}\longrightarrow I^{\otimes j+1}_{m+2}\otimes I_{m}^{\otimes n-j-1}

as the composition

I^{\otimes n+1}_{m+2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}^{\otimes{n}}\otimes r^{m}}I_{m+2}^{\otimes n}\otimes I_{2}\xrightarrow{\rho^{n}_{m+2,j}}I^{\otimes j+1}_{m+2}\otimes I_{m}^{\otimes n-j-1}.

This is a digraph map, as it is the composition of two digraph maps.

Notation 2.4.5
.

For
0\leq j\leq n-1
, and
1<i\leq n
, we define

(2.4.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:I^{\otimes j}_{m+2}\otimes I_{m}^{\otimes n-j}\longrightarrow I^{\otimes j+1}_{m+2}\otimes I_{m}^{\otimes n-j},\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle i<j+1;

(2.4.10)

\displaystyle\hat{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:I^{\otimes j+1}_{m+2}\otimes I_{m}^{\otimes n-j-1}\longrightarrow I^{\otimes j+1}_{m+2}\otimes I_{m}^{\otimes n-j},

\displaystyle i>j+1,

by the formulas

(2.4.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tilde{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})&=(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1},\varepsilon(m+2),v_{i},\dots,v_{n});\\
\hat{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})&=(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1},\varepsilon m,v_{i},\dots,v_{n}).\end{aligned}

Lemma 2.4.6
.

For
0\leq j\leq n-1
,
m\geq 2
even, and
1\leq i\leq n+1
, we have the following properties:

(i)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}=\tilde{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}\bar{\rho}^{n-1}_{m+2,j-1}
if
i<j+1\leq n
;

(ii)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}=\hat{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}\bar{\rho}^{n-1}_{m+2,j}
if
j+1<i\leq n
;

(iii)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{j+1,\varepsilon}
factors through
\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}
;

(iv)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{n+1,1}=\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}
;

(v)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{n+1,0}=\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j+1}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j-1}
.

Proof.

For (i) and (ii), when
i\leq n
, we compute that

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho^{n}_{m+2,j}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1},\varepsilon(2m+2),v_{i},\ldots,v_{n-1},r^{m}(v_{n}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1},\varepsilon(m+2),v_{i},\ldots,v_{j-1},r^{r^{m}(v_{n})}(v_{j}),r^{2}(v_{j+1}),\ldots,r^{2}(v_{n-1})),&&i<j+1;\\
&(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j},r^{r^{m}(v_{n})}(v_{j+1}),r^{2}(v_{j+2}),\ldots,r^{2}(\varepsilon(m+2)),\ldots,r^{2}(v_{n-1})),&&i>j+1,\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tilde{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}\rho^{n-1}_{m+2,j-1}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1},r^{m}(v_{n})),&&i<j+1;\\
&\hat{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}\rho^{n-1}_{m+2,j}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1},r^{m}(v_{n})),&&i>j+1.\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tilde{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}\bar{\rho}^{n-1}_{m+2,j-1}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}),&&i<j+1;\\
&\hat{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}\bar{\rho}^{n-1}_{m+2,j}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}),&&i>j+1.\end{aligned}\right.

For (iii), we compute

(2.4.12)

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{j+1,\varepsilon}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho^{n}_{m+2,j}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j},\varepsilon(m+2),v_{j+1},\ldots,v_{n-1},r^{m}(v_{n}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j},r^{r^{m}(v_{n})}(\varepsilon(m+2)),r^{2}(v_{j+1}),\ldots,r^{2}(v_{n-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j},0,r^{2}(v_{j+1}),\ldots,r^{2}(v_{n-1})),&&\varepsilon=0;\\
&(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j},r^{r^{m}(v_{n})}(2m+2),r^{2}(v_{j+1}),\ldots,r^{2}(v_{n-1})),&&\varepsilon=1.\end{aligned}\right.

It is straightforward that
\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{j+1,0}
factors through
\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}
. For
\varepsilon=1
, and
m\geq 2
, we know that for
m\leq v_{n}\leq m+2
we have

(2.4.13)

r^{r^{m}(v_{n})}(m+2)=r^{2}(m+2)=m,

which implies that
\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{j+1,1}
factors through
\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}
as well. Finally, for (iv) and (v), we compute that

(2.4.14)

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{n+1,\varepsilon}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})

\displaystyle=\rho^{n}_{m+2,j}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},r^{m}(\varepsilon(m+2)))

\displaystyle=\rho^{n}_{m+2,j}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},2\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j},r^{2\varepsilon}(v_{j+1}),r^{2}(v_{j+2}),\ldots,r^{2}(v_{n}))

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j+1},r^{2}(v_{j+2}),\ldots,r^{2}(v_{n})),&&\varepsilon=0;\\
&(v_{1},\ldots,v_{j},r^{2}(v_{j+1}),\ldots,r^{2}(v_{n})),&&\varepsilon=1.\end{aligned}\right.

Therefore we obtain the desired results.
∎

Remark 2.4.7
.

[
CK24
, Prop.4.8]
incorrectly states that for
i<j+1
the maps
\overline{\lambda}^{n}_{m,j}\partial_{i,\varepsilon}
and
\overline{\rho}^{n}_{m,j}\partial_{i,\varepsilon}
factor through
\overline{\lambda}^{n-1}_{m,j}
and
\overline{\rho}^{n-1}_{m,j}
respectively. As proved in Lemma
2.4.6
(i), they should factor through
\overline{\lambda}^{n-1}_{m,j-1}
and
\overline{\rho}^{n-1}_{m,j-1}
instead. And it is important for the constructions of
X_{n,j}
(see Definition
2.4.8
): just because the degree is shifted down by one, so that
j-1\leq n-2
, the face cube of the form (i) in Lemma
2.4.6
factoring through
\bar{\rho}^{n-1}_{m+2,j-1}
will be contained in
X_{n,j}
according to the second assumption of Definition
2.4.8
.

Definition 2.4.8
.

We define a sequence of cubical subsets as follows. Let
X_{0,0}
be the image of
N_{m}G
in
N_{m+2}G
under
(r^{2})^{*}
. For
n>0
, and
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let
X_{n,j}
be the cubical subset of
N_{m+2}G
generated by

(1)

X_{0,0}
,

(2)

for
k<n
,
h\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
, given any map
x:I^{\otimes h+1}_{m+2}\otimes I^{\otimes k-h-1}_{m}\to G
, the
(k+1)
-cube
x\overline{\rho}^{k}_{m+2,h}
of
N_{m+2}G
,

(3)

for
i\leq j
, for any
x:I_{m+2}^{\otimes i}\otimes I^{\otimes n-i}_{m}\to G
, the
(n+1)
-cube
x\overline{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,i-1}
.

It is straightforward from the definition that
X_{n,j}
is a cubical subset of
X_{n,j+1}
, and
X_{n,n}
is a cubical subset of
X_{n+1,1}
. Therefore, we constructed a sequence of inclusions of cubical subsets

(2.4.15)

X_{0,0}\hookrightarrow X_{1,1}\hookrightarrow X_{2,1}\hookrightarrow\dots\hookrightarrow X_{n,j}\hookrightarrow\dots

of
N_{2m+2}G.

Lemma 2.4.9
.

The cubical set
N_{m+2}G
is the colimit of the sequence (
2.4.15
).

Proof.

It suffices to show that
X_{n,n}
contains all
n
-cubes of
N_{m+2}G
. For any
n
cube
x:I^{\otimes n}_{m+2}\to G
, by Lemma
2.4.6
(v), we have

(2.4.16)

x\overline{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,n-1}\partial^{n+1,0}=x(\mathrm{id}_{m+2}^{\otimes n})=x.

Therefore
x
is a face of
x\overline{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,n-1}
, which is contained in
X_{n,n}
by definition.
∎

Lemma 2.4.10
.

For
1\leq j\leq n
, the cubical subset
X_{n,j}
defined in Definition
2.4.8
contains all the
n
-cubes that factor through
\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}
.

Proof.

For any such
n
-cube, it is a composition of
\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}
followed by some map
y:I^{\otimes j}_{m+2}\otimes I^{\otimes n-j}_{m}\to G
. By Lemma
2.4.6
(v), we know that

(2.4.17)

y\overline{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j-1}\partial^{n+1,0}=y(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}).

Therefore, it is a face of the
(n+1)
-cube
y\overline{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j-1}
, which is contained in
X_{n,j}
by the assumption (3) of Definition
2.4.8
.
∎

Lemma 2.4.11
.

For
n\geq 2
,
1\leq j\leq n-1
, and any map
x:I^{\otimes j+1}_{m+2}\otimes I^{\otimes n-j-1}_{m}\to G
, the restriction of the
(n+1)
-cube

(2.4.18)

x\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}:\Box^{n+1}\to N_{m+2}G

to the horn
\sqcap^{n+1}_{n+1,0}
factors through the inclusion
X_{n,j}\hookrightarrow N_{m+2}G
.

Proof.

We need to show that for any
(i,\varepsilon)\neq(n+1,0)
the corresponding face cube
x\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}
is in
X_{n,j}
. By Lemma
2.4.6
, we know that it is one of the forms from (i) to (iv). For (i), we have

(2.4.19)

x\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}=(x\tilde{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon})\bar{\rho}^{n-1}_{m+2,j-1},

therefore by assumption (2) of Definition
2.4.8
it is contained in
X_{n,j}
. Through a similar deduction we can prove for the form (ii). For (iii) and (iv), the face cube factors through
\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}
, so by Lemma
2.4.10
we proved that it is contained in
X_{n,j}
as well.
∎

Lemma 2.4.12
.

For
n\geq 0
, and any map
x:I_{m+2}\otimes I_{m}^{\otimes n}\to G
, the restriction of the
(n+2)
-cube

(2.4.20)

x\bar{\rho}^{n+1}_{m+2,0}:\Box^{n+2}\to N_{m+2}G

to the horn
\sqcap^{n+2}_{n+2,0}
factors through the inclusion
X_{n,n}\hookrightarrow N_{m+2}G
.

Proof.

We first prove the case for
n=0
, by Lemma
2.4.6
, all of the
(1,\varepsilon)
-faces and
(2,1)
-face factor through
r^{2}
, so they are indeed contained in
X_{0,0}
. For
n\geq 1
, we need to show that for any
(i,\varepsilon)\neq(n+2,0)
the corresponding face cube
x\bar{\rho}^{n+1}_{m+2,0}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}
is contained in
X_{n,n}
. Again, by Lemma
2.4.6
we know that it is one of the forms from (ii) to (iv). For (ii), we have

(2.4.21)

x\bar{\rho}^{n+1}_{m+2,0}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}=(x\hat{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon})\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,0},

therefore by assumption (3) of Definition
2.4.8
it is contained in
X_{n,n}
. For (iii) and (iv), the face cube factors through
(r^{2})^{\otimes n+1}
, so it is contained in
X_{0,0}
, then contained in
X_{n,n}
as well.
∎

Proposition 2.4.13
.

For even
m\geq 2
and any digraph
G
, the map

(2.4.22)

(r^{2})^{*}:N_{m}G\longrightarrow N_{m+2}G

is anodyne.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.4.9
, we have

N_{m+2}G=\mathrm{colim}(X_{0,0}\hookrightarrow X_{1,1}\hookrightarrow X_{2,1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow X_{n,j}\hookrightarrow\cdots),

where the sequence of inclusions of cubical subsets is as in Definition
2.4.8
. It suffices to show that each of the inclusions is anodyne. There are two types to consider:

1)

for
n>1
,
1\leq j\leq n-1
, the inclusion
X_{n,j}\hookrightarrow X_{n,j+1}
;

2)

for
n\geq 0
the inclusion
X_{n,n}\hookrightarrow X_{n+1,1}
.

For type 1), let

(2.4.23)

S_{n,j+1}:=\{x:I^{\otimes j+1}_{m+2}\otimes I^{\otimes n-j-1}_{m}\to G\mid x(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j+1}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j-1})\notin X_{n,j}\}.

By Lemma
2.4.11
, together with the assumption (3) of Definition
2.4.8
, we obtain the following commutative square:

(2.4.24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{x\in S_{n,j+1}}\sqcap^{n+1}_{n+1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{x\in S_{n,j+1}}\Box^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n,j+1}.}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\overline{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}|_{\sqcap}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\overline{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}}

We want to show that it is a pushout square. On the one hand, by comparing the definition of
X_{n,j}
and
X_{n,j+1}
, we know that the only generating cubes of
X_{n,j+1}
that are not contained in
X_{n,j}
are exactly of the form
x\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}
for
x\in S_{n,j+1}
, and they are one-to-one corresponding to elements
x\in S_{n,j+1}
. Indeed, for any
x,x^{\prime}\in S_{n,j+1}
such that
x\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}=x^{\prime}\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}
, by Lemma
2.4.6
(iv) we have

(2.4.25)

x(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j})=x\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{n+1,1}=x^{\prime}\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}\partial^{n+1,1}=x^{\prime}(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}),

which implies that
x=x^{\prime}
since
\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n-j}
is an epimorphism. On the other hand, by Lemma
2.4.6
(v), any such
(n+1)
-cube
x\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}
is not contained in
X_{n,j}
since one of its face cube is not contained in
X_{n,j}
by the construction of
S_{n,j+1}
. As a result, the commutative square (
2.4.24
) is a pushout square. Since anodyne maps are stable under coproducts and pushouts, we proved that
X_{n,j}\hookrightarrow X_{n,j+1}
is anodyne.

For type 2), for
n\geq 0
, let

(2.4.26)

S_{n+1,1}:=\{x:I_{m+2}\otimes I_{m}^{\otimes n}\to G\mid x(\mathrm{id}\otimes(r^{2})^{\otimes n})\notin X_{n,n}\}.

Through a similar deduction and using Lemma
2.4.12
, we will obtain the following pushout square:

(2.4.27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{x\in S_{n+1,1}}\sqcap^{n+2}_{n+2,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{x\in S_{n+1,1}}\Box^{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n+1,1}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\overline{\rho}^{n+1}_{m+2,0}|_{\sqcap}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\overline{\rho}^{n+1}_{m+2,0}}

Therefore we proved that the inclusion
X_{n,n}\hookrightarrow X_{n+1,0}
is anodyne as well. Finally, the result follows from that fact that anodyne maps are stable under transfinite compositions.
∎

Proposition 2.4.14
.

For any digraph
G
, the map
r^{*}:N_{1}G\hookrightarrow N_{2}G
is anodyne.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
2.4.13
and Theorem 4.12 of
[
CK24
]
, but with technical differences. We need to prove through the same strategy but replacing
r^{2}
by
r
and
\rho^{n}_{m+2,j},\bar{\rho}^{n}_{m+2,j}
by parallel constructions. Here we provide a sketch of the proof.

For
n\geq 1
,
0\leq j\leq n-1
, we construct a map

(2.4.28)

\rho^{n}_{j}:I^{\otimes n}_{2}\otimes I_{1}\to I^{\otimes j+1}_{2}\otimes I^{\otimes n-j-1}_{1}

as

\rho^{n}_{2}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n},v_{n+1})=\begin{cases}(v_{1},\dots,v_{j+1},r(v_{j+2}),\dots r(v_{n}))&v_{n+1}=0\\
(v_{1},\dots,v_{j},r(v_{j+1}),\dots r(v_{n}))&v_{n+1}=1.\end{cases}

One can easily check that there is an arrow
v_{j+1}\to r(v_{j+1})
, and therefore
\rho^{n}_{j}
is well-defined. Then We can define
\overline{\rho}^{n}_{2,j}
as the composite

(2.4.29)

I^{\otimes n+1}_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n}\otimes r}I_{2}^{\otimes n}\otimes I_{1}\xrightarrow{\rho^{n}_{j}}I^{\otimes j+1}_{2}\otimes I^{\otimes n-j-1}_{1}.

A version of Lemma
2.4.6
can be proved, showing that

(i)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{j}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}=\tilde{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}\bar{\rho}^{n-1}_{j-1}
if
i<j+1\leq n
;

(ii)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{j}\partial^{i,\varepsilon}=\hat{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}\bar{\rho}^{n-1}_{j}
if
j+1<i\leq n
;

(iii)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{j}\partial^{j+1,\varepsilon}
factors through
\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes r^{\otimes n-j}
;

(iv)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{j}\partial^{n+1,1}=\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j}\otimes r^{\otimes n-j}
;

(v)

\bar{\rho}^{n}_{j}\partial^{n+1,0}=\mathrm{id}^{\otimes j+1}\otimes r^{\otimes n-j-1}
.

where
\tilde{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\partial}^{i,\varepsilon}
are defined similarly in an obvious way. One can further define a sequence of inclusions of cubical subsets

(2.4.30)

X_{0,0}\hookrightarrow X_{1,1}\hookrightarrow X_{2,1}\hookrightarrow X_{2,2}\hookrightarrow X_{3,1}\hookrightarrow\cdots

of
N_{2}G
similar to Definition
2.4.8
using (
2.4.28
), whose colimit is
N_{2}G
. Using (i) to (v), one can similarly show the horn-factorization properties parallel to Lemma
2.4.11
and Lemma
2.4.12
. Finally, by adding generating cubes through horn inclusions step-by-step, one can show that
r^{*}:N_{1}G\to N_{2}G
is anodyne, parallel to Proposition
2.4.13
.
∎

Proposition 2.4.15
.

For even
m\geq 2
and any digraph
G
, the map

(2.4.31)

(l^{2})^{*}:N_{m}G\longrightarrow N_{m+2}G

is anodyne.

Proof.

Since
m
is even, there is a digraph isomorphism
f_{m}:I_{m}\to I_{m}
sending
i
to
m-i
. This isomorphism induces an isomorphism of cocubical digraphs

I_{m}^{\otimes*}\cong(I_{m}^{\otimes*})^{\mathrm{op}}
, which induces an isomorphism

(2.4.32)

f^{*}_{m}:N_{m}(G^{\mathrm{op}})\cong N_{m}G.

Note that the diagram

(2.4.33)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{m+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{m+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{m}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{2}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{2}}

is commutative.
Therefore, we obtain a commutative diagram

(2.4.34)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m}(G^{\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m+2}(G^{\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m+2}G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(r^{2})^{*}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(l^{2})^{*}}

Hence the statement follows from Proposition
2.4.13
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.4.1
.

The statement follows from the fact that
N_{\infty}G
can be defined as the colimit of the sequence

(2.4.35)

N_{1}G\xrightarrow{r^{*}}N_{2}G\xrightarrow{(l^{2})^{*}}N_{4}G\xrightarrow{(r^{2})^{*}}N_{6}G\xrightarrow{(l^{2})^{*}}N_{8}G\xrightarrow{(r^{2})^{*}}\dots,

that anodyne maps are closed under transfinite compositions and Propositions
2.4.13
,
2.4.14
and
2.4.15
.
∎

2.5.
Cubical weak equivalences

In this subsection we prove the isomorphism
A_{*}(G)\cong\pi_{*}(N_{\infty}G)
and give several equivalent characterizations of cubical weak equivalences.

Lemma 2.5.1
.

Let
X
be a pointed cubical set with finite number of non-degenerate cubes. Then

(2.5.1)

[X,-]_{*}:\mathsf{cSet}_{*}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Set}_{*}

commutes with filtered colimits.

Proof.

It is easy to see that a pointed cubical set with a finite number of non-degenerate cubes is a compact object of
\mathsf{cSet}_{*}.
Then the result follows from the fact that
[X,Y]_{*}
is equal to the coequaliser

(2.5.2)

\mathsf{cSet}_{*}((X\otimes\Box^{1})/(\{x_{0}\}\otimes\Box^{1}),Y)\rightrightarrows\mathsf{cSet}_{*}(X,Y),

and that
(X\otimes\Box^{1})/(\{x_{0}\}\otimes\Box^{1})
is also a compact object.
∎

Lemma 2.5.2
.

For a pointed cubical set
X
, a pointed digraph
G
and a natural number
m\geq 1
, there is a natural isomorphism

(2.5.3)

[X,N_{m}G]_{*}\cong[|X|_{m},G]_{*}.

Proof.

Note that
|X\otimes\Box^{1}|_{m}\cong|X|_{m}\otimes I_{m}
and, since
|-|_{m}
is left adjoint,

(2.5.4)

|(X\otimes\Box^{1})/(\{x_{0}\}\otimes\Box^{1})|_{m}\cong(|X|_{m}\otimes I_{m})/(\{x_{0}\}\otimes I_{m}).

Then the statement follows from the adjunction
|-|_{m}\dashv N_{m}
, the fact that
[X,Y]_{*}
is isomorphic to the coequaliser (
2.5.2
), and
[H,G]_{*}
is isomorphic to the coequaliser

(2.5.5)

\mathsf{DGra}_{*}((H\otimes I_{m})/(\{h_{0}\}\otimes I_{m}),G)\rightrightarrows\mathsf{DGra}_{*}(H,G)

(Lemma
1.1.28
).
∎

Proposition 2.5.3
.

For any pointed digraph
G
and
n\geq 0
, there is a natural isomorphism

(2.5.6)

A_{n}(G)\cong\pi_{n}(N_{\infty}G).

Proof.

Using that
S^{n}=\Box^{n}/\partial\Box^{n}
has finite number of non-degenerate cubes, Lemma
2.5.1
and Lemma
2.5.2
, we obtain

(2.5.7)

[S^{n},N_{\infty}G]_{*}\cong\underset{m}{\mathrm{colim}}\>[S^{n},N_{4m}G]_{*}\cong\underset{m}{\mathrm{colim}}\>[S^{n}_{4m},G]_{*}.

Therefore
\pi_{n}(N_{\infty}G)\cong A_{n}(G).

∎

Definition 2.5.4

(Cubical weak equivalence)
.

A digraph map
\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
is called a cubical weak equivalence if for any vertex
g
of
G
and
n\geq 0
, the map
A_{n}(G,g)\to A_{n}(G^{\prime},\varphi(g))
is an isomorphism.

Proposition 2.5.5
.

Let
\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
be a digraph map. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\varphi
is a cubical weak equivalence;

(2)

N_{\infty}\varphi
is a weak equivalence of cubical sets;

(3)

N_{1}\varphi
is a weak equivalence of cubical sets.

Proof.

(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)
. It follows from Proposition
2.5.3
and the fact that a morphism of Kan complexes is a weak equivalence if and only if it induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups (Corollary
2.1.17
).

(2)\Leftrightarrow(3).
It follows from Theorem
2.4.1
and the 2-out-of-3 property for weak equivalences.
∎

Corollary 2.5.6
.

Cubical weak equivalences satisfy 2-out-of-3 property and closed under (possibly infinite) coproducts.

Proof.

It follows from the fact that weak equivalences of cubical sets satisfy these properties (see Corollary
2.1.9
) and
N_{1}
preserves filtered colimits and coproducts.
∎

2.6.
Calculus of fractions

In this subsection we recall the idea of calculus of fractions from
[
Cis19
, §7.2]
that allows to compute mapping spaces in localizations of
\infty
-categories.

Definition 2.6.1

(Right calculus of fractions)
.

Let
C
be an
\infty
-category,
W
be a class of morphisms of
C
and
x
be an object of
C
. A right calculus of fractions at
x
(see
[
Cis19
, Def.7.2.2,7.2.6 and 7.2.7]
) is a functor
\pi:I\to C
from an
\infty
-category
I
such that

(a)

I
has a terminal object
i_{0}
and
\pi(i_{0})=x
;

(b)

\pi
sends any map
i\to i_{0}
to
W
;

(c)

the colimit

\underset{i\in I^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>C(\pi(i),-)=\mathrm{colim}(I^{\mathrm{op}}\to C^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(C,\mathsf{Spc}))

treated as a functor
C\to\mathsf{Spc}
sends
W
to isomorphisms in
\mathsf{Spc}
.

Remark 2.6.2
.

In Definition
2.6.1
we combine
[
Cis19
, Def.7.2.6]
with the remark in
[
Cis19
, Def.7.2.7]
saying that the fact that the map
\pi:I\to C
is
W
-local is equivalent to the property (c) in our definition. Here we also use that, since
i_{0}
is terminal in
I
, the functor
I_{/i_{0}}\to I
is an equivalence and the colimit of the functor
(I_{/i_{0}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(C,\mathsf{Spc})
is isomorphic to the colimit of the functor
I^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(C,\mathsf{Spc})
.

Theorem 2.6.3

(
[
Cis19
, Th.7.2.8]
)
.

Let
C
be an
\infty
-category,
W
be a class of morphisms of
C
,

(2.6.1)

\gamma:C\longrightarrow C[W^{-1}]

be the localization functor, and
x
be an object of
C
. Assume that
\pi:I\to C
is a right calculus of fractions at
x
. Then there is an isomorphism

(2.6.2)

\underset{i\in I^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>C(\pi(i),-)\cong C[W^{-1}](x,-)

in the category of functors
C\to\mathsf{Spc}
.

Remark 2.6.4
.

Note that there is a more general version of Theorem
2.6.3
in an unpublished preprint
[
ACK25
]
.

2.7.
Derived cubical nerve functor

In this subsection we show that the derived cubical nerve functor
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
induced by
N_{1}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
is isomorphic to the mapping space from the point
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}(*,-)
.

Definition 2.7.1

(
\infty
-category of digraphs)
.

The
\infty
-category of digraphs is defined as the localization (in the sense of
\infty
-categories) by the class of cubical weak equivalences
W_{\sf cub}

(2.7.1)

\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}=\mathsf{DGra}[W_{\sf cub}^{-1}].

We will treat
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
as an
\infty
-category with the trivial structures of an
\infty
-category of fibrant and cofibrant objects. Further we identify the
\infty
-category of spaces with the localization (in the sense of
\infty
-categories) of the category of cubical sets

(2.7.2)

\mathsf{Spc}=\mathsf{cSet}[W^{-1}].

Since the functor
N_{1}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
sends cubical weak equivalences to weak equivalences of cubical sets, it induces a functor of
\infty
-categories that we denote by

(2.7.3)

N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Spc}

and call the derived cubical nerve functor. Note that, since
N_{1}G\to N_{\infty}G
is anodyne,
N
can be also described as a functor induced by
N_{\infty}
.

Remark 2.7.2
.

Note that
a digraph map
\varphi
is a cubical weak equivalence if and only if
N_{1}\varphi
is a weak equivalence, if and only if
N\varphi
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Spc}
. Therefore
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
is conservative.

Theorem 2.7.3
.

There is a natural isomorphism

(2.7.4)

N\cong\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}(*,-)

of functors
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
, where
*
is the point digraph.

Proof.

Here we use Theorem
2.6.3
. We claim that

(2.7.5)

I_{1}^{\otimes\bullet}:\Box\longrightarrow\mathsf{DGra}

is a right calculus of fractions at
*
. We need to check the properties (a),(b),(c) from Definition
2.6.1
. (a):
\Box
has the terminal object
[1]^{0}
and
I_{1}^{\otimes 0}=*
. (b): The maps
I_{1}^{\otimes n}\to*
are cubical weak equivalences because
I_{1}^{\otimes n}
is contractible.

Let us prove (c). We denote by
i:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Spc}
the inclusion of the category of sets to the
\infty
-category of spaces. Then for a cubical set
X:\Box^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
we have

(2.7.6)

\mathrm{colim}\>iX\cong X,

where
X
it treated as an object of
\mathsf{Spc}

[
ACK25
, Cor.3.14]
. Moreover, this isomorphism is natural in
X
. Therefore, the colimit of the discrete cubical space
\mathsf{DGra}(I_{1}^{\otimes\bullet},G):\Box^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Spc}
is
NG
and we have

(2.7.7)

N\cong\underset{\Box^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>\mathsf{DGra}(I_{1}^{\otimes\bullet},-).

It follows that
\mathrm{colim}_{\Box^{\mathrm{op}}}\>\mathsf{DGra}(I_{1}^{\otimes\bullet},-)
sends weak equivalences to isomorphisms in
\mathsf{Spc}
.

Then the statement follows from Theorem
2.6.3
and the isomorphism (
2.7.7
).
∎

3.
Finite homotopy limits

In this section, we describe finite limits of the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
by introducing a structure of a category of fibrant objects on
\mathsf{DGra}
, similar to
[
CK24
, Th. 5.9]
, whose weak equivalences are cubical weak equivalences and whose fibrations are the so-called “cubical fibrations.” As a corollary, we obtain that
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
admits all finite limits and that the derived cubical nerve functor
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
preserves finite limits. To provide examples of cubical fibrations, we show that the 2-coverings introduced in
[
DIMZ24
]
are cubical fibrations.

3.1.
Cubical fibrations

In this subsection we introduce cubical fibrations of digraphs and prove that the maps
G\to PG\to G\times G
give a factorization of the diagonal map into a cubical weak equivalence followed by a cubical fibration.

Definition 3.1.1

(Cubical fibration of digraphs)
.

A digraph map
p:G\to H
is called cubical fibration, if
N_{\infty}p:N_{\infty}G\to N_{\infty}H
is a fibration of cubical sets.

Proposition 3.1.2
.

A digraph map
p:G\to H
is a cubical fibration if and only if, for any commutative square

(3.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{4m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{4m}^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\hookleftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

there exists even
k\geq 0
and a map
I^{\otimes n}_{4(m+k)}\to G
such that the diagram

(3.1.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{4(m+k)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{4m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{4(m+k)}^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{4m}^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{2k}_{*}}
\hookleftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\hookleftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{2k}_{*}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

is commutative.

Proof.

Since
\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}
and
\Box^{n}
are compact objects in
\mathsf{cSet}
and
N_{\infty}G=\mathrm{colim}\>N_{4m}G
, we obtain that
N_{\infty}p:N_{\infty}G\to N_{\infty}H
is a fibration if and only if, for any commutative square

(3.1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{4m}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Box^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{4m}H,}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\hookleftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

there is
k\geq 0
and a map
\Box^{n}\to N_{4(m+k)}G
such that the diagram

(3.1.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{4m}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{4(m+k)}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Box^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{4m}H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{4(m+k)}H}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

\rightarrow

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c^{2k})^{*}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c^{2k})^{*}}

is commutative. Then the result follows from the adjunction
|\cdot|_{m}\dashv N_{m}
.
∎

Notation 3.1.3
.

For a digraph
G
and its vertex
g
, we denote by

(3.1.5)

PG=P_{\sf St}G,\hskip 28.45274pt\Omega(G,g)=\Omega_{\sf St}(G,g)

the path and loop digraphs associated with the standard nice subcategory of shrinkings.

Proposition 3.1.4
.

For a digraph
G
, the maps

(3.1.6)

G\longrightarrow PG\longrightarrow G\times G

give a factorization of the diagonal map into a cubical weak equivalence followed by a cubical fibration.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.2.3
we have an isomorphism

(3.1.7)

N_{4m}(\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(I_{4m},G))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\Box^{1},N_{4m}G).

Therefore,

(3.1.8)

\displaystyle N_{\infty}(PG)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{colim}_{m}\>N_{4m}(\mathrm{colim}_{k}\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(I_{4k},G))

(3.1.9)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{colim}_{m}\mathrm{colim}_{k}\>N_{4m}(\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(I_{4k},G))

(3.1.10)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{colim}_{m}\>N_{4m}(\mathrm{Hom}^{\otimes}(I_{4m},G))

(3.1.11)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{colim}_{m}\>\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\Box^{1},N_{4m}G)

(3.1.12)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\Box^{1},N_{\infty}G).

It follows that after applying
N_{\infty}
to the maps
G\to PG\to G\times G
we obtain the factorization of the diagonal map

(3.1.13)

X\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\Box^{1},X)\longrightarrow X\times X,

into a weak equivalence followed by a fibration
[
CK23
, Prop.2.22]
, where
X=N_{\infty}G
.
∎

3.2.
Category of fibrant objects

In this subsection we show that the category
\mathsf{DGra}
equipped with the classes of cubical weak equivalences and cubical fibrations is a category of fibrant objects. Using this we prove that
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
admits finite limits and that the functor
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
preserves finite limits.

Definition 3.2.1

(
\infty
-category with weak equivalences and fibrations)
.

Following Cisinski
[
Cis19
, Def. 7.4.6; 7.4.12]
, we define an
\infty
-category with weak equivalences and fibrations as an
\infty
-category
C
equipped with two classes of morphisms
W
and
F
, called weak equivalences and fibrations, such that the following holds.

(1)

W
and
F
contain all isomorphisms and closed under composition.

(2)

C
has a terminal object
1

(an object
x
is called fibrant if a map
x\to 1
is a fibration).

(3)

W
satisfies
2
-out-of-
3
property.

(4)

For any fibrant objects
x,y,y^{\prime}
any fibration
f:x\to y
and any morphism
v:y^{\prime}\to y
the pullback
x\times_{y}y^{\prime}
exists and the map
f^{\prime}:x\times_{y}y^{\prime}\to y^{\prime}
is a fibration. Moreover, if
f\in F\cap W
, then
f^{\prime}\in F\cap W.

(5)

Any map
u:x\to y
, where
y
is fibrant, factors as a weak equivalence
w:x\to x^{\prime}
followed by a fibration
f:x^{\prime}\to y.

Morphisms from
W\cap F
are called acyclic fibrations.

If
C
is an ordinary category equipped with two classes of morphisms
W
and
F
, we say that it is a category with weak equivalences and cofibrations if its nerve
\mathsf{Ner}(C)
is an
\infty
-category of weak equivalences and cofibrations.

An
\infty
-category with weak equivalences and cofibrations is defined dually.

Definition 3.2.2

(
\infty
-category of fibrant objects)
.

An
\infty
-category of fibrant objects is an
\infty
-category with weak equivalences and fibrations such that all its objects are fibrant
[
Cis19
, Def. 7.5.7]
. An (ordinary) category of fibrant objects is a category with weak equivalences and fibrations such that all its objects are fibrant. Note that any
\infty
-category with finite colimits can be equipped with a structure of the category of fibrant objects, where weak equivalences are isomorphisms and all morphisms are fibrations. This structure will be called the trivial structure of a category of fibrant objects. An
\infty
-category of cofibrant objects is defined dually.

The following lemma is well-known but we could not find a reference in the
\infty
-categorical setting, so we give it with a proof.

Lemma 3.2.3

(Factorization lemma, cf.
[
Bro73
]
)
.

Let
C
be an
\infty
-category equipped with two classes of morphisms
W
and
F
satisfying axioms (1)-(4) from Definition
3.2.1
. Assume that all objects are fibrant. Then
C
is an
\infty
-category of fibrant objects if and only if the following condition is satisfied:

(5’)

for any object
y
the diagonal map
y\to y\times y
factors as a weak equivalence
w:y\to p
followed by a fibration
f:p\to y\times y
.

Moreover, for a map
u:x\to y
the factorization of
u
to a weak equivalence followed by a fibration is defined by morphisms

(3.2.1)

x\xrightarrow{w_{u}}p_{u}\xrightarrow{{\sf pr}_{2}f_{u}}y

where
w^{\prime}:x\to p_{u}
and
f_{u}:p_{u}\to x\times y
are defined by the diagram

(3.2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\times y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\times y}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}_{x},u)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{u}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\times\mathrm{id}_{y}}

where
p_{u}
is the pullback.

Proof.

Since all objects are fibrant, a product of two any objects
x\times y
exist and projections
{\sf pr}_{1}:x\times y\to x
and
{\sf pr}_{2}:x\times y\to y
are fibrations. In the diagram (
3.2.2
) the map
f_{u}:p_{u}\to x\times y
is a fibration. Therefore, the map
{\sf pr}_{2}f_{u}:p_{u}\to y
is a fibration. Note that the square

(3.2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf pr}_{1}f_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf pr}_{1}f}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

is also a pullback. Using the 2-out-of-3 property and the fact that
w
is a weak equivalence, and the isomorphism
{\sf pr}_{1}fw\cong\mathrm{id}_{y}
, we obtain that
{\sf pr}_{1}f
is an acyclic fibration.
Therefore,
{\sf pr}_{1}f_{u}
is also an acyclic fibration. Using the 2-out-of-3 property again, and the isomorphism
{\sf pr}_{1}f_{u}w_{u}\cong\mathrm{id}_{x}
, we obtain that
w_{u}
is a weak equivalence. Therefore the map
u:x\to y
factors as a weak equivalence
w_{u}:x\to p_{u}
followed by a fibration
{\sf pr}_{2}f_{u}:p_{u}\to y
as required.
∎

Definition 3.2.4

(Left exact functor)
.

Let
C
and
D
be
\infty
-categories of fibrant objects.
A functor
F:C\to D
is called left exact
[
Cis19
, Def. 7.5.2]
, if

(1)

it preserves the terminal object;

(2)

it preserves fibrations and acyclic fibrations;

(3)

it preserves pullbacks along fibrations.

Proposition 3.2.5
.

The category
\mathsf{DGra}
equipped with classes of cubical weak equivalences and cubical fibrations is a category of fibrant objects. Moreover, the functor
N_{\infty}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
is left exact.

Proof.

Let us prove that
\mathsf{DGra}
is a category of fibrant objects.
(1) Cubical weak equivalences and cubical fibrations contain all isomorphisms and closed under compositions.
(2)
\mathsf{DGra}
has a terminal object
*
.
(3) Cubical weak equivalences satisfy 2-out-of-3 property.
(4) The functor
N_{\infty}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
preserves pullbacks (Proposition
2.2.5
). Therefore the fact that cubical fibrations and acyclic cubical fibrations are closed under pullbacks follows from the fact that fibrations and acyclic fibrations of cubical sets are closed under pullbacks. (5’) Let us use Lemma
3.2.3
. For any digraph
G
,
N_{\infty}G
is a Kan complex (Theorem
2.3.1
). It follows that any digraph is fibrant. Then the assumption of Lemma
3.2.3
follows from Proposition
3.1.4
.

The fact that
N_{\infty}
is left exact follows from Propositions
2.2.5
and
2.5.5
and the definition of cubical fibrations.
∎

Theorem 3.2.6
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
admits finite limits and the localization functor

\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}

is a left exact functor of
\infty
-categories of fibrant objects. Moreover, the functor
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
preserves finite limits.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
3.2.5
and
[
Cis19
, Prop.7.5.6]
.
∎

Example 3.2.7
.

For any pointed digraph
G=(G,g)
, the square

(3.2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

is a pullback square in
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
. Indeed, consider the diagram,

(3.2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{g}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times\mathrm{id}}

where all squares are pullbacks (Lemma
1.4.3
). By Lemma
3.2.3
the map
*\to P_{g}G
is a cubical weak equivalence and the map
P_{g}G\to G
is a cubical fibration. Then applying the left exact localization functor
\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
, we obtain that the square (
3.2.4
) is a pullback square.

3.3.
Long exact sequence

In this subsection we show that the main result of
[
LWYZ24
]
about the long exact sequence of cubical homotopy groups follows from our results.

Proposition 3.3.1
.

Let

(3.3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

be a pullback square in
\mathsf{DGra}
, where
T
is a digraph such that
T\to*
is a cubical weak equivalence. Assume that either
T\to H
or
G\to H
is a cubical fibration. Then, for any consistent choice of basepoints, there is a long exact sequence

(3.3.2)

\dots\to A_{1}(F)\to A_{1}(G)\to A_{1}(H)\to A_{0}(F)\to A_{0}(G)\to A_{0}(H).

Proof.

Since either
G\to H
is a cubical fibration, or
T\to H
is a cubical fibration, we obtain that the image of this square in
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
is a pullback. Since
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
preserves pullbacks, applying
N
to this square we obtain a pullback in the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{Spc}
, where
NT\simeq*
. Therefore
NF\to NG\to NH
is a fiber sequence. Then the result follows from the long sequence of homotopy groups for spaces and the isomorphism
A_{*}(-)\cong\pi_{*}(N(-))
.
∎

Definition 3.3.2
.

For any pointed digraphs
G=(G,g)
and
H=(H,h)
, and a pointed digraph map
\varphi:G\to H
, we define
P_{\varphi}
as a pullback

(3.3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\varphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{h}H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

where
P_{h}H
is defined in (
3.2.5
). Note that by Lemma
3.2.3
the map
*\to P_{h}H
is a cubical weak equivalence and the map
P_{h}H\to H
is a cubical fibration.

Corollary 3.3.3

(
[
LWYZ24
, Th.1.1]
)
.

For any pointed digraphs
G=(G,g)
and
H=(H,h)
and a pointed digraph map
\varphi:G\to H
there is a long exact sequence

(3.3.4)

\dots\to A_{1}(P_{\varphi})\to A_{1}(G)\to A_{1}(H)\to A_{0}(P_{\varphi})\to A_{0}(G)\to A_{0}(H).

3.4.
2-covering digraphs

In this subsection we recall the definition
\ell
-covering digraphs introduced in
[
DIMZ24
]
and show that
2
-covering digraphs are cubical fibrations. As a corollary we obtain that for a 2-covering
p:G\to H
we have an isomorphism
A_{n}(G,g)\cong A_{n}(H,p(g))
for
n\geq 2
.

Definition 3.4.1

(
1
-covering digraph)
.

A digraph map
p:G\to H
is called
1
-covering, if for any commutative square

(3.4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{k}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

where
k\in\{0,1\}
, there exists a unique map
I_{1}\to G
making the diagram commutative.

Definition 3.4.2

(Directed distance)
.

For two vertices
g,g^{\prime}
of a digraph
G
, we denote by
{\sf dist}(g,g^{\prime})
the infimum of lengths of paths from
g
to
g^{\prime}
. Note that for any digraph map
\varphi:G\to H
and any
g,g^{\prime}\in G
we have

(3.4.2)

\mathsf{dist}(\varphi(g),\varphi(g^{\prime}))\leq\mathsf{dist}(g,g^{\prime}).

Definition 3.4.3

(
\ell
-covering digraph)
.

For a digraph
G
and
k\geq 1
, we denote by
D_{k}(G)
the digraph with the same set of vertices such that there is an arrow
g\to g^{\prime}
in
D_{k}(G)
if there is a path of length at most
k
from
g
to
g^{\prime}
in
D
.

(3.4.3)

E(D_{k}(G))=\{(g,g^{\prime})\mid\mathsf{dist}(g,g^{\prime})\leq k\}.

For
\ell\geq 1
, a digraph map
p:G\to H
is called
\ell
-covering, if the induced map
D_{k}(p):D_{k}(G)\to D_{k}(H)
is a
1
-covering for all
1\leq k\leq\ell
.

Proposition 3.4.4

(
[
DIMZ24
, Prop.3.3]
)
.

For a digraph map
p:G\to H
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

p
is an
\ell
-covering.

(2)

D_{\ell}(p):D_{\ell}(G)\to D_{\ell}(H)
is an
1
-covering and
G=D_{\ell}(p)^{-1}(H).

(3)

p
is
1
-covering and if
(g_{0},\dots,g_{n})
and
(g^{\prime}_{0},\dots,g^{\prime}_{m})
are paths in
G
such that
0\leq n,m\leq\ell
and either
g_{0}=g_{0}^{\prime}
and
p(g_{n})=p(g^{\prime}_{m})
, or
p(g_{0})=p(g^{\prime}_{0})
and
g_{n}=g^{\prime}_{m}
, then
g_{0}=g^{\prime}_{0}
and
g_{n}=g^{\prime}_{m}
.

(4)

For any
h,h^{\prime}\in H
such that
\mathsf{dist}_{H}(h,h^{\prime})\leq\ell
, there is a unique bijection between fibers
\alpha:p^{-1}(h)\to p^{-1}(h^{\prime})
such that for any
g\in p^{-1}(h)
we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G}(g,\alpha(g))=\mathsf{dist}_{H}(h,h^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{dist}_{G}(g,g^{\prime})>\ell
for any
g^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(h)\setminus\{\alpha(g)\}
.

Lemma 3.4.5
.

Let
A\subseteq B
be a (not necessarily induced) subdigraph such that the following holds.

(1)

For any
b\in B
, there exists
a\in A
with an arrow
a\to b
in
B
.

(2)

For any
b\in B
and
a,a^{\prime}\in A
such that there are arrows
a\to b
and
a^{\prime}\to b
, there exists
a^{\prime\prime}\in A
with arrows
a^{\prime\prime}\to a
and
a^{\prime\prime}\to a^{\prime}
in
A
.

(3)

For any arrow
b\to b^{\prime}
in
B
, there exists an arrow
a\to a^{\prime}
in
A
together with arrows
a\to b
and
a^{\prime}\to b^{\prime}
in
B
.

Then any
2
-covering
p:G\to H
has a unique right lifting property with respect to the inclusion
A\hookrightarrow B
.

Remark 3.4.6
.

In the formulation of Lemma
3.4.5
some of the arrows can be degenerate. For example, in (1), if
b\in A
, then
a
can be equal to
b
. In (2),
a^{\prime\prime}
can be equal to
a
or to
a^{\prime}
. In (3),
b
or
b^{\prime}
can be in
A
and in this case we can have
a=b
or
a^{\prime}=b^{\prime}
. Also note that in (3) it is possible that
b,b^{\prime}\in A
but the arrow
b\to b^{\prime}
exists in
B
but not in
A
.

Proof of Lemma
3.4.5
.

Let
\alpha:A\to G
and
\beta:B\to H
are digraph maps such that
\beta|_{A}=p\alpha
. We need to show that there is a unique digraph map
\tilde{\alpha}:B\to G
such that
\tilde{\alpha}|_{A}=\alpha
and
p\tilde{\alpha}=\beta
. Uniqueness follows from (1): if
\tilde{\alpha}
exists and
a\to b
is an arrow from a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
, then
\alpha(a)\to\tilde{\alpha}(b)
is the unique lifting of the arrow
\beta(a)\to\beta(b)
.

Let us construct
\tilde{\alpha}.
For any arrow
a\to b
in
B
, where
a\in A
and
b\in B
, we define
\tilde{\alpha}_{a}(b)\in G
so that
\alpha(a)\to\tilde{\alpha}_{a}(b)
is the lifting of the arrow
\beta(a)\to\beta(b)
. Using (2) and Proposition
3.4.4
(3) we obtain that
\tilde{\alpha}_{a}(b)
does not depend on
a.
So we can denote it by
\tilde{\alpha}(b)
. Using (1) we obtain that
\tilde{\alpha}(b)
is defined for all
b\in B
. Let us show that
\tilde{\alpha}:B\to G
is a digraph map. Take an arrow
b\to b^{\prime}
in
B
. Then by (3) there exist an arrow
a\to a^{\prime}
in
A
together with arrows
a\to b
and
a^{\prime}\to b^{\prime}
in
B
. Consider a lifting
\tilde{\alpha}(b)\to g^{\prime}
of

\beta(b)\to\beta(b^{\prime})
. Then
\alpha(a)\to\alpha(a^{\prime})\to\tilde{\alpha}(b^{\prime})
and
\alpha(a)\to\alpha(b)\to g^{\prime}
are the liftings of
\beta(a)\to\beta(a^{\prime})\to\beta(b^{\prime})
and
\beta(a)\to\beta(b)\to\beta(b^{\prime})
respectively. By Proposition
3.4.4
(3), it follows that
g^{\prime}=\tilde{\alpha}(b^{\prime}),
and hence, there is an arrow
\tilde{\alpha}(b)\to\tilde{\alpha}(b^{\prime}).

∎

Proposition 3.4.7
.

Any 2-covering is a cubical fibration.

Proof.

Take a
2
-covering
p:G\to H
. It is sufficient to show that
p
has the unique right lifting property with respect to the map
|\sqcap^{n}_{i,\varepsilon}|_{m}\to I_{m}^{\otimes n}
for any even
m\geq 2
, any
n\geq 0
and any
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
. Assume that
\varepsilon=1
. If we set

(3.4.4)

A_{k}=(I_{m}^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes I_{k}\otimes I_{m}^{\otimes(n-i)})\cup|\sqcap^{n}_{i,1}|_{m},

we obtain a filtration

(3.4.5)

|\sqcap^{n}_{i,1}|_{m}=A_{0}\subseteq A_{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq A_{m}=I_{m}^{\otimes n}

such that, for any
1\leq k\leq m
, the inclusion
A_{k-1}\subseteq A_{k}
either satisfies the assumption of Lemma
3.4.5
, or its dual. Then
p
has the unique right lifting property with respect to
|\sqcap^{n}_{i,1}|_{m}\to I_{m}^{\otimes n}.
The case
\varepsilon=0
is similar.
∎

Corollary 3.4.8
.

For any
2
-covering
p:G\to H
and any
g\in G
,
p
induces an injective map
A_{1}(G,g)\to A_{1}(H,p(g))
and isomorphisms

(3.4.6)

A_{n}(G,g)\cong A_{n}(H,p(g)),\ \ n\geq 2.

Proof.

It follows from Corollary
3.3.1
, Proposition
3.4.7
and the fact that the fiber of a
2
-covering is discrete.
∎

Example 3.4.9
.

For any
n\geq 3
, we denote by
C_{n}
the directed
n
-cycle that can be defined as the Cayley digraph of
\mathbb{Z}/n
with respect to the generator
1
. We also denote by
C_{\infty}
the Cayley digraph of
\mathbb{Z}
with respect to the generator
1
. Then the map

(3.4.7)

C_{\infty}\longrightarrow C_{n}

is a 2-covering. It is easy to see that
C_{\infty}=\mathrm{colim}\>J_{n}
, where
J_{n}
is the induced subgraph on the set of vertices
\{-n,\dots,n\}
. Therefore
A_{*}(C_{\infty},0)=\mathrm{colim}\>A_{*}(J_{n},0)\simeq*
.
It follows that

(3.4.8)

A_{i}(C_{n},0)\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z},&i=1,\\
0,&i\neq 1.\end{cases}

4.
Nerve theorem for in-closed covers

In this section, we introduce the notions of in-closed and out-closed subdigraphs, as well as the notions of in-closure and out-closure of a subdigraph. We prove a version of the nerve theorem for covers of a digraph by in-closed subdigraphs, which is a very efficient tool for computing the homotopy type of
N_{1}G
for certain classes of digraphs
G
. We also show that if a digraph
G
is the union of two of its in-closed subdigraphs,
G=H\cup H^{\prime}
, then
N_{1}G=N_{1}H\sqcup_{N_{1}(H\cap H^{\prime})}N_{1}H^{\prime}
. This result is used in the following section to construct a structure of a category of cofibrant objects.

Definition 4.0.1

(In-closed subdigraph)
.

An induced subdigraph
H\subseteq G
is called in-closed if every arrow of
G
with target in
H
also has its source in
H
. Dually
H\subseteq G
is out-closed if arrow of
G
with source in
H
also has its target in
H
. Note that
H\subseteq G
is in-closed if and only if the subdigraph
G\setminus H
(induced subdigraph on
V(G)\setminus V(H)
) is out-closed. For a subdigraph
H\subseteq G
we denote by

(4.0.1)

\mathsf{Out}(H)\subseteq G,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathsf{In}(H)\subseteq G

the least out-closed subdigraph and the least in-closed subdigraph containing
H
respectively. They are called in-closure and out-closure of
H
. If we want to specify
G
, we will use the notations
\mathsf{Out}_{G}(H)
and
\mathsf{In}_{G}(H)
. Note that for any digraph map
\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
we have

(4.0.2)

f(\mathsf{Out}_{G}(H))\subseteq\mathsf{Out}_{G^{\prime}}(f(G)),\hskip 28.45274ptf(\mathsf{In}_{G}(H))\subseteq\mathsf{In}_{G^{\prime}}(f(H)).

Also note that the preimage of an in-closed (resp. out-closed) subdigraph is in-closed (resp. out-closed).

Remark 4.0.2
.

Hepworth and Roff in
[
HR25
]
and
[
HR24
]
denote the out-closure
\mathsf{Out}(H)
by
rH
and call it “reach of
H
”.

Definition 4.0.3

(In-closed cover)
.

An in-closed (resp. out-closed) cover of a digraph
G
is a family
(H_{i})_{i\in I}
of its in-closed (resp. out-closed) subdigraphs such that
G=\bigcup_{i\in I}H_{i}.

Proposition 4.0.4
.

Let
G
be a digraph and
(H_{i})_{i\in I}
be it in-closed (resp. out-closed) cover and let
A
be a digraph which is equal to in-closure (resp. out-closure) of its one vertex. Then

(4.0.3)

\mathrm{Hom}(A,G)=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathrm{Hom}(A,H_{i})

(here we identify
\mathrm{Hom}(A,H_{i})
with its image in
\mathrm{Hom}(A,G)
).

Proof.

Let
a
be the vertex of
A
such that
A=\mathsf{In}_{A}(\{a\}).
Then for any map
\varphi:A\to G
we have
\varphi(A)=\varphi(\mathsf{In}_{A}(a))\subseteq\mathsf{In}_{G}(\varphi(a)).
Since
G=\bigcup_{i}H_{i},
there exists
i
such that
\varphi(a)\in H_{i}.
Therefore
\varphi(A)\subseteq\mathsf{In}_{G}(\varphi(a))\subseteq H_{i}.

∎

Corollary 4.0.5
.

Let
G
be a digraph and
(H_{i})_{i\in I}
be it in-closed (resp. out-closed) cover. Then

(4.0.4)

N_{1}G=\bigcup_{i\in I}N_{1}H_{i}.

Proof.

If follows from the fact that
I_{1}^{\otimes k}
is in-closure of its vertex
(1,\dots,1)
and out-closure of it’s vertex
(0,\dots,0)
.
∎

Definition 4.0.6

(Nerve of a collection of subsets or subdigraphs)
.

Let
X
be a set and
\mathcal{U}=(U_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of its subsets. For a finite subset
\sigma\subseteq I,
we set

U_{\sigma}=\bigcap_{i\in\sigma}U_{i}.

The nerve complex
\mathsf{Ner}(\mathcal{U})
of this family is the (abstract) simplicial complex defined by

(4.0.5)

\mathsf{Ner}(\mathcal{U})=\{\sigma\subseteq I\mid U_{\sigma}\neq\emptyset,\>\sigma\text{ is non-empty finite}\}.

For a digraph
G
and a family of its induced subdigraphs
\mathcal{H}=(H_{i})_{i\in I}
we denote by
\mathsf{Ner}(\mathcal{H})
the nerve-complex of the family
(V(H_{i}))_{i\in I}
in the set of vertices
V(G)
.

Proposition 4.0.7

(Nerve theorem for in-closed covers)
.

Let
G
be a digraph and
\mathcal{H}=(H_{i})_{i\in I}
be its in-closed cover such that
N_{1}(\bigcap_{i\in\sigma}H_{i})
is weakly contractible for any
\sigma\in\mathsf{Ner}(\mathcal{H}).
Then there is a homotopy equivalence

(4.0.6)

|N_{1}G|\simeq|\mathsf{Ner}(\mathcal{H})|.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.0.4
we have
N_{1}G=\bigcup_{i\in I}N_{1}H_{i}.
Observe that
\bigcap_{i\in\sigma}N_{1}H_{i}=N_{1}(\bigcap_{i\in\sigma}H_{i})
for any finite
\sigma\subseteq I.
Therefore, the result follows from the nerve lemma for cubical sets (see Proposition
6.0.5
in the Appendix).
∎

Proposition 4.0.8
.

Let
\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
be a morphism of digraphs and
(H_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(H^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
be in-closed covers of
G
and
G^{\prime}
respectively such that
\varphi(H_{i})\subseteq H^{\prime}_{i}.
Assume that for any
i_{0},\dots,i_{n}\in I
the map

(4.0.7)

H_{i_{0}}\cap\dots\cap H_{i_{n}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}H^{\prime}_{i_{0}}\cap\dots\cap H^{\prime}_{i_{n}}

is a cubical weak equivalence. Then
\varphi
is a cubical weak equivalence.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.0.4
we have
N_{1}G=\bigcup_{i\in I}N_{1}H_{i}
and it is easy to see that
N_{1}(H_{i_{0}}\cap\dots\cap H_{i_{n}})=N_{1}H_{i_{0}}\cap\dots\cap N_{1}H_{i_{n}}
. Then the result follows from Proposition
6.0.6
in the Appendix.
∎

Example 4.0.9
.

Consider the digraph
O:=\mathsf{Out}_{I_{4}^{\otimes 2}}(\partial I_{4}^{\otimes 2}).

O:\hskip 14.22636pt\hbox to249.69pt{\vbox to129.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 124.84549pt\lower-64.63821pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-124.84549pt}{-64.63821pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}

{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 144.5 -48.62 L 144.5 -46.9}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{104.42883pt}{-35.13855pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@discardpath@clipped{M 0 -5.96 h 5.36 v 11.92 h -5.36 Z} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.12506pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{57}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{1.0}{-1.0}{0.0}{104.42883pt}{-33.89148pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@discardpath@clipped{M 0 -5.96 h 5.36 v 11.92 h -5.36 Z} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.12506pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{57}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Take its out-closed covering defined by the 4 sets of vertices:

(4.0.8)

\{0,1\}\times V,\ \ \{3,4\}\times V,\ \ V\times\{0,1\},\ \ V\times\{3,4\},

where
V=V(I_{4})=\{0,\dots,4\}
. All these 4 out-closed subdigraphs and their non-empty intersections are contractible. Then the nerve of this cover is the cyclic graph with 4 vertices. Therefore

(4.0.9)

|N_{1}O|\sim S^{1}.

Considering the intersection of this out-closed cover with
\partial I_{4}^{\otimes 2}
. We similarly obtain that
|N_{1}(\partial I_{4}^{\otimes 2})|\sim S^{1}.
Moreover, using Proposition
4.0.8
, we obtain that the inclusion

(4.0.10)

\partial I_{4}^{\otimes 2}\longrightarrow O

is a cubical weak equivalence.

Proposition 4.0.10
.

Let
H,H^{\prime}\subseteq G
be two in-closed subdigraphs such that
G=H\cup H^{\prime}
. Then the squares

(4.0.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\cap H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}(H\cap H^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

are pushout squares in the category of digraphs and the category of cubical sets respectively.

Proof.

First we prove that the left-hand square is a pushout. It is sufficient to prove that any arrow of
G
is either in
H
or in
H^{\prime}
. Suppose for contradiction that an arrow
(u,v)
of
G
belongs neither to
H
nor to
H^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality we can assume that
u\in G\setminus H
and
v\in G\setminus H^{\prime}.
Then
v\in H^{\prime}
and this contradicts to the fact that
H^{\prime}
is in-closed. Therefore
G=H\sqcup_{H\cap H^{\prime}}H^{\prime}.

Corollary
4.0.5
implies that
N_{1}G=N_{1}H\cup N_{1}H^{\prime}.
One checks that
N_{1}H\cap N_{1}H^{\prime}=N_{1}(H\cap H^{\prime}).
Then
N_{1}G=N_{1}H\sqcup_{N_{1}(H\cap H^{\prime})}N_{1}H^{\prime}.

∎

5.
Homotopy colimits

In this section, we describe colimits of the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
by introducing
a structure of a homotopy cocomplete category of cofibrant objects on
\mathsf{DGra}
, whose weak equivalences are cubical weak equivalences and whose cofibrations are the
so-called “in-closed cofibrations.” As a corollary, we obtain that
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
is cocomplete and the derived cubical nerve functor
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
is colimit-preserving. In the end of this section we use these results to prove the main result of the paper that
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of spaces.

5.1.
In-closed inclusions

In this subsection we study inclusions of in-closed subdigraphs which are later used in the definition in-closed cofibrations. In particular, we show that in-closed inclusions form a saturated class of morphisms and that the functor
N_{1}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
preserves pushouts of two in-closed inclusions.

Definition 5.1.1

(Induced inclusion)
.

An injective digraph map is called induced inclusion if its image is an induced subdigraph.

Definition 5.1.2

(In-closed inclusion)
.

An induced inclusion of digraphs is called in-closed (resp. out-closed) inclusion if its image is an in-closed (resp. out-closed) subdigraph.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

(1)

Induced inclusions are stable under pushouts.

(2)

In-closed inclusions are stable under pushouts.

Proof.

(1) Let
H\subseteq G
be an induced subdigraph and
\varphi:H\to H^{\prime}
be a map. Set
G^{\prime}=G\sqcup_{H}H^{\prime}
(see the description of the pullback in Remark
1.1.12
). We need to prove that the subdigraph
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is induced. Take an arrow
(v,u)\in E(G^{\prime})
, where
v,u\in V(H^{\prime})
. We need to show that
(v,u)\in E(H^{\prime})
. Assume that
(v,u)
is from the image of
E(G)\to V(G^{\prime})\times V(G^{\prime})
. Then there exist
(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})\in E(G)
such that
\varphi^{\prime}(v^{\prime})=v
and
\varphi^{\prime}(u^{\prime})=u.
It follows that
v^{\prime},u^{\prime}\in V(H)
. Since
H
is induced,
(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})\in E(H)
. Therefore,
(v,u)\in E(H^{\prime})
.

(2) Now we assume that
H\subseteq G
is in-closed and prove that
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is in-closed. Take an arrow
(v,u)\in E(G^{\prime})
, where
u\in V(H^{\prime}).
We need to prove that
v\in V(H^{\prime})
. If
(v,u)\in E(H^{\prime}),
there is nothing to prove. Assume that
(v,u)
is from the image of
E(G)\to E(G^{\prime}).
Then there exists
(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})\in E(G)
such that
\varphi^{\prime}(v^{\prime})=v
and
\varphi^{\prime}(u^{\prime})=u.
Since
u\in V(H^{\prime}),
we have
u^{\prime}\in V(H)
. Using that
H
is in-closed, we obtain
v^{\prime}\in V(H).
Therefore,
v\in V(H^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 5.1.4
.

(1)

Induced inclusions are closed with respect to transfinite compositions.

(2)

In-closed inclusions are closed with respect to transfinite compositions.

Proof.

(1). We can always replace in-closed inclusions with actual inclusions of subdigraphs. Therefore we can assume that we have an
\alpha
-sequence of subdigraphs i.e a collection of subdigraphs
(G_{\beta}\subseteq G)_{\beta<\alpha},
such that
G_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\gamma}
for
\beta\leq\gamma<\alpha
; for any limit ordinal
\lambda
we have
G_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}
and
G=\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}G_{\beta}
. We need to show that if
G_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta+1}
is induced, then
G_{0}\subseteq G
is induced. Assume that
g^{\prime}\to g
is an arrow of
G
such that
g^{\prime},g\in G_{0}
. Consider the least
\beta
such that
g^{\prime}\to g
is in
G_{\beta}
. We need to prove that
\beta=0
. If
\beta
is the limit ordinal, then
G_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\gamma<\beta}G_{\gamma}
, then we can find
\gamma<\beta
such that
g^{\prime}\to g
is in
G_{\gamma}
, which is a contradiction. If
\beta=\gamma+1
, then
g^{\prime},g\in G_{\gamma}
and, using that
G_{\gamma}\subseteq G_{\gamma}
is induced we obtain that
g^{\prime}\to g
is in
G_{\gamma}
, which is a contradiction. Therefore
\beta=0
.

(2). We need to show that if
G_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta+1}
is in-closed, for any
\beta
such that
\beta+1<\alpha
, then
G_{0}\subseteq G
is in-closed. Assume that there is an arrow
g^{\prime}\to g
of
G
with
g\in G_{0}
. Consider the least
\beta
such that
g^{\prime}\to g
is in
G_{\beta}
. We need to show that
\beta=0
. The ordinal
\beta
can not be a limit ordinal because in this case
G_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\gamma<\beta}G_{\gamma}
and we would find
\gamma<\beta
such that the arrow is in
G_{\gamma}
. If
\beta=\gamma+1
for some
\gamma,
then
g\in G_{\gamma}
, and using that
G_{\gamma}\subseteq G_{\gamma+1}
is in-closed we obtain that
g^{\prime}\to g
is in
G_{\gamma}
, which is a contradiction. Therefore
\beta=0
.
∎

Lemma 5.1.5
.

(1)

Induced inclusions are closed under retracts.

(2)

In-closed inclusions are closed under retracts.

Proof.

(1) Let
H\subseteq G
be an induced subdigraph. Assume that
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
and
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}\cap H
are subdigraphs and there is a retraction
r:G\to G^{\prime}
such that
r(H)\subseteq H^{\prime}
. We need to prove that
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is induced.
If
h^{\prime}_{1},h^{\prime}_{2}\in H^{\prime}
such that there is an arrow
h^{\prime}_{1}\to h_{2}^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
, then this arrow is in
H
, because
H
is induced. Then the image of this arrow of
H
under
r
is the arrow
h^{\prime}_{1}\to h^{\prime}_{2}
of
H^{\prime}
. Hence
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is induced.

(2) Assume that
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}\to h^{\prime}
is an arrow in
G^{\prime}
. Since
H\subseteq G
is in-closed,
g^{\prime}\in H.
Then
g^{\prime}=r(g^{\prime})\in H^{\prime}.

∎

Definition 5.1.6

(Saturated class of morphisms)
.

A class of morphisms of a category is called saturated if it is closed under pushouts, transfinite compositions and retracts.

Proposition 5.1.7
.

(1)

Induced inclusions form a saturated class of morphisms.

(2)

In-closed inclusions form a saturated class of morphisms.

Proof.

It follows from Lemmas
5.1.3
,
5.1.4
,
5.1.5
.
∎

Proposition 5.1.8
.

Let
i:H\to G
and
j:H\to G^{\prime}
be in-closed inclusions (resp. out-closed). Then

(5.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}G^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}(G\sqcup_{H}G^{\prime})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

is pushout square in the category of cubical sets.

Proof.

Set
G^{\prime\prime}=G\sqcup_{H}G^{\prime}.
Since
i
and
j
are in-closed inclusions, the maps
H\to G^{\prime\prime}
,
G\to G^{\prime\prime}
and
G^{\prime}\to G^{\prime\prime}
are also in-closed inclusions. Therefore we can identify them with their images
G,G^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime\prime}
which are in-closed subdigraphs such that
G^{\prime\prime}=G\cup G^{\prime}
and
G\cap G^{\prime}=H.
Then the result follows from Proposition
4.0.10
.
∎

Corollary 5.1.9
.

Let
H\subseteq G
be an in-closed subdigraph and
O=\mathsf{Out}(H).
Then the square

(5.1.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}(O\setminus H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}O}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}(G\setminus H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

is a pushout square in the category of cubical sets.

Proposition 5.1.10
.

Let
H\subseteq G
be an in-closed subdigraph and
\varphi:H\to H^{\prime}
be a digraph map. Set
O=\mathsf{Out}_{G}(H),

O^{\prime}=O\sqcup_{H}H^{\prime}
and

Proof.

We assume

are inclusions, we can identify

and

H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
