∎

5.2.
Directed deformation retracts

In this subsection we introduce a notion of a directed deformation retract which is later used in the definition of in-closed cofibrations.

Definition 5.2.1

(Directed deformation retract)
.

Let
H\subseteq G
be an in-closed subdigraph. It is called directed deformation retract, if there is a morphism of digraphs
\eta:G\to G
such that the following holds:

(DDR1)

there is an arrow
\eta(g)\to g
, for any
g\in G
;

(DDR2)

if
h\in H
, then
\eta(h)=h
;

(DDR3)

if
g\in G\setminus H
, then for any
h\in H
we have

{\sf dist}(h,g)={\sf dist}(h,\eta(g))+1.

In particular,
{\sf dist}(h,g)=\infty
if and only if
{\sf dist}(h,\eta(g))=\infty
.

An in-closed inclusion
i:H\to G
is called directed deformation retract, if the image
i(H)\subseteq G
is a directed deformation retract.

Example 5.2.2
.

Let
\tilde{\mathbb{N}}
be the digraph, whose vertices are natural numbers and arrows have the form
n\to n+1
. Then the one-vertex subdigraph
H=\{0\}\subseteq\tilde{\mathbb{N}}
is a directed deformation retract. The map
\eta:\tilde{\mathbb{N}}\to\tilde{\mathbb{N}}
is defined by
\eta(n)=\max\{0,n-1\}
. It is indicated by dashed arrows:

(5.2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

Another example is given by
G=I_{1}\otimes I_{1}
and
H=\{(0,0)\}
.

(5.2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf 00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 01}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 10}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 11}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

For any digraph
G,

G\otimes\{0\}
is a directed deformation retract of
G\otimes I_{1}.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

Let
H\subseteq G
be an in-closed subdigraph and
\eta:G\to G
be a morphism of digraphs satisfying (DDR1) and (DDR2). Then (DDR3) is equivalent to the fact that, for any
g\in G
, there is a natural number
n=n(g)\geq 1
such that

(a)

\eta^{n}(g)\in H
;

(b)

for any
h\in H
such that there is a path from
h
to
g
, there is a shortest path from
h
to
g
that factors via

\eta^{n}(g)\to\eta^{n-1}(g)\to\dots\to g.

Proof.

Assume that
\eta
satisfies (DDR3). Take some
g\in G
. By induction, we obtain that, for any
i\geq 0
such that
\eta^{i-1}(g)\in G\setminus H,
and any
h\in G
, we have

(5.2.3)

\mathsf{dist}(h,g)=\mathsf{dist}(h,\eta^{i}(g))+i

Therefore, there exists
n
such that
\eta^{i}(g)\in H.
We denote by
n=n(g)\geq 1
the smallest
n
such that
\eta^{n}(g)\in H.
Then we obtain
\mathsf{dist}(\eta^{n}(g),g)=n
. Hence
\eta^{n}(g)\to\dots\to\eta(g)\to g
is a shortest path. We also have
\mathsf{dist}(h,g)=\mathsf{dist}(h,\eta^{n}(g))+n
for any
h\in H.
Then there is a path from
h
to
g
that factors via
\eta^{n}(g)\to\dots\to\eta(g)\to g
.

Now assume that (a),(b) are satisfied. Take some
g\in G\setminus H
and consider the corresponding number
n=n(g)
. If
\mathsf{dist}(h,g)=\infty
, then
\mathsf{dist}(h,\eta(g))=\infty
, because there is an arrow
\eta(g)\to g
. If
\mathsf{dist}(h,g)<\infty
, there is a shortest path of the form
h=x_{0}\to\dots\to x_{k}\to\eta^{n}(g)\to\dots\to\eta(g)\to g.
Then
\mathsf{dist}(h,g)=\mathsf{dist}(h,\eta(g))+1
.
∎

Proposition 5.2.4
.

A directed deformation retract is a cubical weak equivalence.

Proof.

Denote by
G_{n}\subseteq G
the induced subdigraph consisting of vertices
g
such that
\eta^{n}(g)\in H.
Then
G_{0}=H
and
G=\bigcup G_{n}
. Since there exists arrows
\eta(g)\to g,
the restriction
\eta_{n}:G_{n}\to G_{n}
of
\eta
is homotopic to the identity map
\eta_{n}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{G_{n}}
. Therefore its
n
-th power is also homotopic to the identity
\eta_{n}^{n}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{G_{n}}
. If we denote by
i_{n}:H\to G_{n}
the inclusion and by
r_{n}:G_{n}\to H
the map defined by
r_{n}(g)=\eta_{n}^{n}(g),
we obtain
r_{n}i_{n}=\mathrm{id}_{H}
and
i_{n}r_{n}=\eta_{n}^{n}\sim\mathrm{id}_{G_{n}}.
Note that for any vertex
h\in H
, the map
i_{n}:(H,h)\to(G_{n},h)
and
r_{n}:(G_{n},h)\to(H,h)
are pointed morphisms and the constructed homotopy equivalence
i_{n}r_{n}\sim\mathrm{id}_{G_{n}}
is also pointed. Therefore,
i_{n}
induces an isomorphism
A_{n}(H,h)\cong A_{n}(G_{n},h)
, and hence,
i_{n}
is a cubical weak equivalence.

Since cubical weak equivalences satisfy 2-out-of-3 property, the inclusions
G_{n}\to G_{n+1}
are also weak equivalences. Therefore the maps
N_{1}(G_{n})\to N_{1}(G_{n+1})
are anodyne. Since anodyne maps are closed under transfinite compositions, and
N_{1}
preserves filtered colimits, the map
N_{1}(H)\to N_{1}(G)
is also anodyne. Hence the map
H\to G
is a cubical weak equivalence.
∎

Lemma 5.2.5
.

Directed deformation retracts are closed under pushouts.

Proof.

Let
H\subseteq G
be a directed deformation retract. Consider a map
\varphi:H\to H^{\prime}
and set
G^{\prime}=G\sqcup_{H}H^{\prime}
and
O^{\prime}=O\sqcup_{H}H^{\prime}.
We will identify
H^{\prime}
with its image in
G^{\prime}
(Remark
1.1.12
). Therefore, we need to show that
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is a directed deformation retract. We construct
\tilde{\eta}:G^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}
by the universal property of the pushout

(5.2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\varphi}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\varphi}\eta}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\eta}}

Since in-closed inclusions are closed under pushouts,
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is in-closed. So we need to check that
\eta^{\prime}
satisfies (DDR1)-(DDR3). (DDR1) and (DDR2) are obvious.

Let us prove (DDR3) using Lemma
5.2.3
. We take some
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}\setminus H^{\prime}
, denote its preimage by
g\in G\setminus H
and set
n=n(g^{\prime}):=n(g)
. Then
\tilde{\eta}^{n}(g^{\prime})=\varphi(\eta^{n}(g))\in H^{\prime}
.
Take some
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
such that there exists a path from
h^{\prime}
to
g^{\prime}
. It is sufficient to show that there is a shortest path from
h^{\prime}
to
g^{\prime}
that factors via the path
\tilde{\eta}^{n}(g^{\prime})\to\dots\to\tilde{\eta}(g^{\prime})\to g^{\prime}
. Take some shortest path
h^{\prime}=x_{0}\to\dots\to x_{k}=g^{\prime}
. Choose the last vertex of this path
x_{l}
, which is in
H^{\prime}.
Using the isomorphism
G\setminus H\cong G^{\prime}\setminus H^{\prime}
we obtain that the path
x_{l+1}\to\dots\to x_{k}
has a unique preimage
y_{l+1}\to\dots\to y_{k}=g
in
G.
The arrow
x_{l}\to x_{l+1}
also has a preimage
y_{l}\to y_{l+1}
in
G
, where
y_{l}\in H.
Then the path
y_{l}\to\dots\to y_{k}=g
is a preimage of a shortest path. Therefore, it is also shortest. In particular
\mathsf{dist}_{G}(y_{l},g)=k-l.
By Lemma
5.2.3
there exists a shortest path
y_{l}=y^{\prime}_{l}\to y^{\prime}_{l+1}\to\dots\to y^{\prime}_{k}=g
in
G
that factors via
\eta^{n}(g)\to\dots\to\eta(g)\to g
. Set
x^{\prime}_{l+i}=\varphi(y^{\prime}_{l+i}).
Then
h^{\prime}=x_{0}\to\dots\to x_{l}\to x^{\prime}_{l+1}\to\dots\to x^{\prime}_{k}=g^{\prime}
is a shortest path that factors via
\tilde{\eta}^{n}(g^{\prime})\to\dots\to\tilde{\eta}(g^{\prime})\to g^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 5.2.6
.

Directed deformation retracts are closed under transfinite compositions.

Proof.

We can always replace in-closed inclusions with actual inclusions of subdigraphs. Therefore we can assume that we have an
\alpha
-sequence of subdigraphs i.e a collection of subdigraphs
(G_{\beta}\subseteq G)_{\beta<\alpha},
such that
G_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\gamma}
for
\beta\leq\gamma<\alpha
; for any limit ordinal
\lambda
we have
G_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}G_{\beta}
and
G=\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}G_{\beta}
. We need to show that if
G_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta+1}
is a directed deformation retract, for any
\beta
such that
\beta+1<\alpha
, then
G_{0}\subseteq G
is a directed deformation retract.

Note that the fact that for any limit ordinal
\lambda
we have
G_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}G_{\beta}
implies that for any
g\in G\setminus G_{0}
there is a unique
\beta=\beta(g)
such that
g\in G_{\beta+1}\setminus G_{\beta}
. We denote by
\eta_{\beta}:G_{\beta+1}\to G_{\beta+1}
the map from the definition of a direct deformation retract with respect to the subdigraph
G_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta+1}
. We need to show that
G_{0}\subseteq G
is a directed deformation retract.

Since in-closed inclusions are closed under transfinite compositions,
G_{\beta}\subseteq G
is in-closed for any
\beta<\alpha
. We define
\eta:G\to G
so that for any vertex
g\in G_{\beta+1}\setminus G_{\beta}
we have
\eta(g)=\eta_{\beta}(g)
, and for
g\in G_{0}
, we have
\eta(g)=g
. Note that for any
g\in G
we have an arrow
\eta(g)\to g
(either degenerate or not).
Let us check that
\eta
is a morphism of digraphs. Consider an arrow
g^{\prime}\to g
in
G
and prove that there is an arrow
\eta(g^{\prime})\to\eta(g)
. If
g\in G_{0}
, then
g^{\prime}\in G_{0}
and
\eta(g^{\prime})=g^{\prime}\to g=\eta(g)
is the required arrow. Now assume that
g\in G\setminus G_{0}
. Take
\beta
such that
g\in G_{\beta+1}\setminus G_{\beta}
. Since
G_{\beta+1}\subseteq G
is in-closed, we obtain
g^{\prime}\in G_{\beta+1}
. If
g^{\prime}\in G_{\beta+1}\setminus G_{\beta}
, then
\eta_{\beta}(g)\to\eta_{\beta}(g^{\prime})
is the required arrow. If
g^{\prime}\in G_{\beta}
, then
1=\mathsf{dist}_{G_{\beta+1}}(g^{\prime},g)=\mathsf{dist}_{G_{\beta+1}}(g^{\prime},\eta_{\beta+1}(g))+1.
Therefore
g^{\prime}=\eta_{\beta+1}(g)
. Hence
\eta(g^{\prime})\to g^{\prime}=\eta(g)
is the required arrow.

We already have the properties (DDR1) and (DDR2).
Let’s check the property (DDR3). Assume that
g\in G\setminus G_{0}
and
h\in G_{0}
. Take
\beta
such that
g\in G_{\beta+1}\setminus G_{\beta}
. Then

(5.2.5)

\displaystyle\mathsf{dist}_{G}(h,g)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{dist}_{G_{\beta+1}}(h,g)

(5.2.6)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{dist}_{G_{\beta+1}}(h,\eta_{\beta+1}(g))+1

(5.2.7)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{dist}_{G}(h,\eta(g))+1.

∎

Lemma 5.2.7
.

Directed deformation retracts are closed under retracts.

Proof.

Lets
H\subseteq G
be a directed deformation retract and
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
and
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
be subdigraphs with a retraction
r:G\to G^{\prime}
such that
r(H)\subseteq H^{\prime}
. We need to prove that
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is a directed deformation retract. Since in-closed inclusions are closed under retracts,
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is in-closed. Let
\eta:G\to G
be a morphism of digraphs from the definition of directed deformation retract. Define
\eta^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}
by the formula
\eta^{\prime}=r\circ\eta|_{G^{\prime}}
. Let us check the properties (DDR1)-(DDR3) for
\eta^{\prime}
with respect to the subdigraph
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
. Take
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}
. Then we have an arrow
\eta(g^{\prime})\to g^{\prime}
. Since
r(g^{\prime})=g^{\prime},
its image is an arrow
r(\eta(g^{\prime}))\to g^{\prime}.
Then (DDR1) is satisfied. If
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
, then
\eta(h^{\prime})=h^{\prime}
and
r(h^{\prime})=h^{\prime}
. Therefore (DDR2) is also satisfied. Take
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}
. Note that, since
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
is a retract, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(h^{\prime},g^{\prime})=\mathsf{dist}_{G}(h,g)
. Therefore

(5.2.8)

\displaystyle\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(h^{\prime},g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{dist}_{G}(h^{\prime},g^{\prime})

(5.2.9)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{dist}_{G}(h^{\prime},\eta(g^{\prime}))+1

(5.2.10)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(r(h^{\prime}),r(\eta(g^{\prime}))+1

(5.2.11)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(h^{\prime},r(\eta(g^{\prime}))+1

Since there is an arrow
r(\eta(g^{\prime}))\to g^{\prime}
, we obtain
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(h^{\prime},g^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(h^{\prime},r(\eta(g^{\prime})))+1
. Then
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(h^{\prime},g^{\prime})=\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(h^{\prime},r(\eta(g^{\prime})))+1
and the property (DDR3) is satisfied.
∎

Proposition 5.2.8
.

Directed deformation retracts form a saturated class of morphisms.

Proof.

It follows from Lemmas
5.2.7
,
5.2.6
,
5.2.5
.
∎

5.3.
In-closed cofibrations

In this subsection we introduce the notion of in-closed cofibrations which is used in the definition of a category of cofibrant objects on
\mathsf{DGra}
, show that the class of in-closed cofibrations is saturated and prove that
N_{1}
sends pushouts along in-closed cofibrations to homotopy pushouts.

Definition 5.3.1

(ODDR-subdigraph, in-closed cofibration)
.

An in-closed subdigraph
H\subseteq G
is called ODDR-subdigraph (out-closure directed deformation retract) if
H
is a directed deformation retract of
\mathsf{Out}(H)
. An in-closed inclusion
i:H\to G
is called in-closed cofibration, if
i(H)\subseteq G
is an ODDR-subdigraph.

Lemma 5.3.2
.

In-closed cofibrations are closed under pushouts.

Proof.

Let
H\subseteq G
be an ODDR-subdigraph and
\varphi:H\to H^{\prime}
be a digraph map. Set
O=\mathsf{Out}_{G}(H)
,
G^{\prime}=G\sqcup_{H}H^{\prime}
and
O^{\prime}=O\sqcup_{H}H^{\prime}
. By Proposition
5.1.10
,
O^{\prime}=\mathsf{Out}_{G^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})
.
Since directed deformation retracts are closed under pushouts,
H^{\prime}\subseteq O^{\prime}
is a directed deformation retract. The statement follows.
∎

Lemma 5.3.3
.

In-closed cofibrations are closed under transfinite compositions.

Proof.

As usual we assume that an
\alpha
-sequence of in-closed inclusions is represented by inclusions of digraphs
(G_{\beta}\subseteq G)_{\beta<\alpha},
such that
G_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\gamma}
for
\beta\leq\gamma<\alpha
; for any limit ordinal
\lambda
we have
G_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}G_{\beta}
; and
G=\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}G_{\beta}
. We assume that
G_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta+1}
are ODDR-subdigraphs, for any
\beta
such that
\beta+1<\alpha
, and prove that
G_{0}\subseteq G
is an ODDR-subdigraph.

Set
O=\mathsf{Out}_{G}(G_{0})
. We define
H_{\beta}
for
\beta<\alpha
so that
H_{0}=G_{0}
,
H_{\beta+1}=\mathsf{Out}_{G_{\beta+1}}(H_{\beta})
and
H_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}H_{\beta}
, for a limit ordinal
\lambda
. We need to prove that
H_{0}\to O
is a directed deformation retract. Since directed deformation retracts are closed under transfinite compositions, it is sufficient to prove that
H_{\beta}\subseteq H_{\beta+1}
is a directed deformation retract; for any limit ordinal
\lambda
we have
H_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}H_{\beta}
; and
O=\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}H_{\beta}
.

First we prove that
H_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta}
is out-closed and that

(5.3.1)

H_{\beta}\cap G_{\beta^{\prime}}=H_{\beta^{\prime}},\hskip 28.45274pt\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta<\alpha.

The proof is by induction on
\beta
. Assume that
\beta=\gamma+1
. Then
H_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta}
is out-closed by the definition. For
\beta^{\prime}\leq\gamma
we have
H_{\gamma}\cap G_{\beta^{\prime}}=H_{\beta^{\prime}}
. Since
H_{\beta^{\prime}}
is out-closed in
G_{\beta^{\prime}}
, we obtain
H_{\beta}\cap G_{\beta^{\prime}}=\mathsf{Out}_{G_{\beta}}(H_{\gamma})\cap G_{\beta^{\prime}}=H_{\beta^{\prime}}
. For the case
\beta^{\prime}=\beta
the equation
H_{\beta}\cap G_{\beta^{\prime}}=H_{\beta^{\prime}}
is obvious. Now assume that
\beta
is a limit ordinal. Then we have
G_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\gamma<\beta}G_{\gamma}
and
H_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\gamma<\beta}H_{\gamma}
. Assume that there is an arrow
g\to g^{\prime}
, where
g\in H_{\beta}
and
g^{\prime}\in G_{\beta}
. Then there exists
\gamma<\beta
such that
g\to g^{\prime}
is in
G_{\gamma}
and there exists
\gamma\leq\gamma^{\prime}<\beta
such that
g\in H_{\gamma^{\prime}}
. Since
H_{\gamma^{\prime}}\cap G_{\gamma}=H_{\gamma}
we obtain
g\in H_{\gamma}.
Using that
H_{\gamma}\subseteq G_{\gamma}
is out-closed, we obtain
g^{\prime}\in H_{\gamma}.
Therefore
H_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta}
is out-closed. The fact that for
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
we have
H_{\beta}\cap G_{\beta^{\prime}}=H_{\beta^{\prime}}
follows from the distributivity

\left(\bigcup_{\beta^{\prime}\leq\gamma<\beta}H_{\gamma}\right)\cap G_{\beta^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{\beta^{\prime}\leq\gamma<\beta}(H_{\gamma}\cap G_{\beta^{\prime}})=H_{\beta^{\prime}}.

Let’s prove that
H_{\beta}\subseteq H_{\beta+1}
is a directed deformation retract. Denote by
\eta_{\beta}:\mathsf{Out}_{G_{\beta+1}}(G_{\beta})\to\mathsf{Out}_{G_{\beta+1}}(G_{\beta})
the map from the definition of a directed deformation retract. We claim that
\eta_{\beta}
can be restricted to a map
\tilde{\eta}_{\beta}:H_{\beta+1}\to H_{\beta+1}
. Indeed, for a vertex
h\in H_{\beta+1}
, we have a path from a vertex of
H_{\beta}
to
h
. The image of this path under
\eta_{\beta}
is a path from the same vertex of
H_{\beta}
to
\eta_{\beta}(h)
. Therefore
\eta_{\beta}(h)\in H_{\beta+1}
. Using that
H_{\beta+1}\cap G_{\beta}=H_{\beta}
, we see that the properties (DDR1)-(DDR3) for
\tilde{\eta}_{\beta}
with respect to the subdigraph
H_{\beta}\subseteq H_{\beta+1}
follow from the properties of
\eta_{\beta}
with respect to the subdigraph
G_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta+1}
.

Let us prove that, for a limit ordinal
\lambda<\alpha
, we have
H_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}H_{\beta}
. Indeed,
H_{\lambda}=H_{\lambda}\cap(\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}G_{\beta})=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}(H_{\lambda}\cap G_{\beta})=\bigcup_{\beta<\lambda}H_{\beta}
.

Let us prove that
O=\bigcup H_{\beta}
. By induction one checks that
H_{\beta}\subseteq O.
Let us prove
O\subseteq\bigcup H_{\beta}
. Take a vertex
o
of
O.
Then there is a path from a vertex of
H_{0}
to
o
.
Take
\beta<\alpha
such that
o\in G_{\beta}
. Then there is a path from a vertex of
H_{\beta}
to
o
. Using that
H_{\beta}\subseteq G_{\beta}
is out-closed, we obtain
o\in H_{\beta}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3.4
.

In-closed cofibrations are closed under retracts.

Proof.

Let
H\subseteq G
be an ODDR-subdigraph. Assume that
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
and
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}\cap H
are subdigraphs such that there is a retraction
r:G\to G^{\prime}
such that
r(H)\subseteq H^{\prime}
. We need to show that
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is an ODDR-subdigraph. Set
O=\mathsf{Out}_{G}(H)
and
O^{\prime}=\mathsf{Out}_{G^{\prime}}(H^{\prime})
. Then
H\subseteq O
is a directed deformation retract. Applying
r
to any path from a vertex of
H
to a vertex of
G
yields a path from a vertex of
H^{\prime}
to a vertex of
G^{\prime}
. Therefore
r(O)\subseteq O^{\prime}
. Then the statement follows from the fact that directed deformation retracts are closed under retracts.
∎

Proposition 5.3.5
.

In-closed cofibrations form a saturated class of maps.

Proof.

It follows from Lemmas
5.3.2
,
5.3.3
,
5.3.4
.
∎

Theorem 5.3.6
.

The functor
N_{1}:\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{cSet}
sends pushouts along in-closed cofibrations to homotopy pushouts.

Proof.

We assume
H\subseteq G
is an ODDR-subdigraph, denote by
i:H\to G
the inclusion. We need to show that
N_{1}
sends pushouts along
i
to homotopy pushouts. Consider a map
\varphi:H\to H^{\prime}
. We set
O=\mathsf{Out}_{G}(H)
,
G^{\prime}=G\sqcup_{H}H^{\prime}
,
O^{\prime}=O\sqcup_{H}H^{\prime}
. Since the maps
H^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}
and
O^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}
are inclusions, we can identify
H^{\prime}
and
O^{\prime}
with their images in
G^{\prime}.
Since in-closed cofibrations are stable under pushouts,
H^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
is an ODDR-subdigraph.
Proposition
5.1.10
implies that the square

(5.3.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}O}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}O^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}G^{\prime}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

a pushout square. Moreover, since
N_{1}O\to N_{1}G
is a cofibration, it is a homotopy pushout square.

Using that
H\subseteq G
and
H^{\prime}\subseteq G
are ODDR-subdigraphs, by Proposition
5.2.4
we obtain the following commutative diagram with vertical maps all cubical weak equivalences.

(5.3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}H^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}O}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{1}O^{\prime}}}
\Leftarrow
\Leftarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

Therefore, the homotopy pushout of the upper row coincides with the homotopy pushout of the bottom row and equal to
N_{1}G^{\prime}
.
∎

5.4.
Category of cofibrant objects

In this subsection we show that
\mathsf{DGra}
equipped with classes of cubical weak equivalences and in-closed cofibrations is a homotopy cocomplete category of cofibrant objects. Using this we prove that
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
admits small colimits and
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
preserves them.

Definition 5.4.1

(Homotopy cocomplete
\infty
-category of cofibrant objects)
.

An
\infty
-category of cofibrant objects
C
is homotopy cocomplete
[
Cis19
, Def. 7.7.2]
if it has small coproducts and the classes of cofibrations and acyclic cofibrations are closed under coproducts. Let
C
and
D
be homotopy cocomplete
\infty
-categories of cofibrant objects. A right exact functor
F:C\to D
is called homotopy cocontinuous, if it preserves coproducts.

Lemma 5.4.2
.

For any digraph
G
, the maps

(5.4.1)

G\sqcup G\longrightarrow G\otimes I_{5}\longrightarrow G

form a factorization of the codiagonal
G\sqcup G\to G
as an in-closed cofibration followed by a cubical weak equivalence.

Proof.

Since
G\otimes I_{5}\to G
is a homotopy equivalence, it is a cubical weak equivalence. The image of the map
G\sqcup G\to G\otimes I_{5}
is
G\otimes\{0,5\}
. Its out-closure is
G\otimes(I_{0,1}\sqcup I_{4,5})
, where
I_{0,1}
and
I_{4,5}
are induced subdigraphs of
I_{5}
spanned by vertices
0,1
and
4,5
respectively. So the result follows from the fact that
G\otimes\{0,5\}\subseteq G\otimes(I_{0,1}\sqcup I_{4,5})
is an ODDR-subdigraph.
∎

Proposition 5.4.3
.

The category of digraphs
\mathsf{DGra}
equipped with the classes of cubical weak equivalences and in-closed cofibrations is a homotopy cocomplete category of cofibrant objects.

Proof.

Let us prove the axioms of a category of cofibrant objects.
\mathsf{DGra}
has an initial object
\emptyset
. Moreover,
\emptyset\to G
is an in-closed cofibration, for any
G
. Therefore all digraphs are cofibrant. Since in-closed cofibrations form a saturated class of maps (Proposition
5.3.5
) they contain all isomorphisms, closed under compositions and pushouts. Cubical weak equivalences satisfy 2-out-of-3 property by Corollary
2.5.6
. The fact that acyclic cofibrations are closed under pushouts follows from Theorem
5.3.6
. For any digraph
G
, the maps
G\sqcup G\to G\otimes I_{5}\to G
form a factorization of the codiagonal
G\sqcup G\to G
as an in-closed cofibration followed by a cubical weak equivalence (Lemma
5.4.2
).
Then by Factorization Lemma (Lemma
3.2.3
) the category
\mathsf{DGra}
is a category of cofibrant objects.

Since in-closed cofibrations form a saturated class, they are closed under coproducts. Cubical weak equivalences are closed under coproducts by Corollary
2.5.6
. Therefore the category of cofibrant objects is homotopy cocomplete.
∎

Theorem 5.4.4
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
is cocomplete, and the localization functor
\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}

is homotopy cocontinuous. Moreover, the derived cubical nerve functor
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
is colimit-preserving.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{DGra}
is a homotopy cocomplete category of cofibrant objects (Proposition
5.4.3
), by
[
Cis19
, Prop.7.7.4]
, we obtain that
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
is cocomplete and
\mathsf{DGra}\to\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
is homotopy cocontinuous.

Consider the functor

\tilde{N}_{1}:\mathsf{DGra}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Spc}

defined as the composition of
N_{1}
with the localization functor
\mathsf{cSet}\to\mathsf{Spc}
. We claim that
\tilde{N}_{1}
is homotopy cocontinuous. First we need to check that it is right exact. To see this, note that
\tilde{N}_{1}
preserves the initial object because it sends the empty digraph to the empty space;
\tilde{N}_{1}
preserves cofibrations and acyclic cofibrations because all morphisms of
\mathsf{Spc}
are cofibrations and
\tilde{N}_{1}
sends acyclic cofibrations to isomorphisms; (3)
\tilde{N}_{1}
sends pushouts along in-closed cofibrations to pushouts in
\mathsf{Spc}
by Theorem
5.3.6
. Then
\tilde{N}_{1}
is homotopy cocontinuous because it preserves coproducts. Therefore the fact that
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
is colimit-preserving follows from
[
Cis19
, Cor.7.7.5]
.
∎

5.5.
Equivalence with the
\infty
-category of spaces

In this subsection we prove our main result that
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of spaces.

Remark 5.5.1

(Universal property
\mathsf{Spc}
)
.

For a cocomplete
\infty
-category
C
, the evaluation at the one-point space defines an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

(5.5.1)

\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathsf{Spc},C)\simeq C,

where
\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{colim}}
denotes the full subcategory in the
\infty
-category of functors spanned by colimit-preserving functors
[
Lur09
, Th.5.1.5.6]
. Moreover, the category of colimit-preserving functors coincide with the full subcategory of left adjoint functors

(5.5.2)

\mathrm{Fun}^{L}(\mathsf{Spc},C)=\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathsf{Spc},C)

(see
[
Lur09
, Not.5.1.5.1]
). In particular, for any object
X
of
C
, there exists a unique (up to isomorphism) adjunction

(5.5.3)

F_{X}:\mathsf{Spc}\leftrightarrows C:G_{X},

where
F_{X}
is the only colimit-preserving functor preserving such that
F_{X}(*)=X
. Note that the isomorphism
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{Spc}}\cong\mathsf{Spc}(*,-)
implies that

(5.5.4)

G_{X}\cong C(X,-).

Definition 5.5.2
.

Since the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
is cocomplete, by the universal property of
\mathsf{Spc}
, there exists a colimit-preserving functor

(5.5.5)

R:\mathsf{Spc}\longrightarrow\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}

such that
R(*)=*
.

Theorem 5.5.3
.

The functor
R:\mathsf{Spc}\to\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}
and the derived cubical nerve functor
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
are mutually inverse equivalences of
\infty
-categories

(5.5.6)

\mathsf{Spc}\simeq\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.7.3
we have
N\cong\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}(*,-).
Then
Remark
5.5.1
implies that the functors

(5.5.7)

R:\mathsf{Spc}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}:N

are adjoint. Since
N
is colimit-preserving (Theorem
5.4.4
),

NR:\mathsf{Spc}\to\mathsf{Spc}
is also colimit-preserving and
NR(*)=*.
Using the equivalence
\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathsf{Spc},\mathsf{Spc})\cong\mathsf{Spc}
, we obtain that the unit
\eta:\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{Spc}}\to NR
is an isomorphism.

Let us check that the counit
\varepsilon:RN\to\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}}
is an isomorphism. Since
N:\mathsf{DGra}_{\infty}\to\mathsf{Spc}
is conservative (Remark
2.7.2
), it is sufficient to prove that
N\varepsilon:NRN\to N
is an isomorphism. This follows from the commutativity of the triangle

(5.5.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces NRN}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{N}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta N}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\varepsilon}

and the 2-out-of-3 property of the class of isomorphisms.
∎

6.
Appendix. Nerve theorem for cubical sets

In this section we prove the nerve theorem for cubical sets which is similar to the nerve theorem for simplicial sets that can be found in
[
IX24
, §12]
.

Definition 6.0.1

(Triangulation of a cubical set)
.

Note that there are well defined maps of simplicial sets

(6.0.1)

\max,\min:\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{1}.

They allow us do define a cocubical simplicial set

(6.0.2)

\Box\longrightarrow\mathsf{sSet},\hskip 28.45274pt[1]^{n}\mapsto(\Delta^{1})^{n}

that induces an adjunction that we denote by

(6.0.3)

T:\mathsf{cSet}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{sSet}:N_{T}.

It is known that this adjunction is a Quillen equivalence
[
DKLS24
, Th.6.26]
,
[
Cis06
, Prop.8.4.30]
. The functor
T
respects the geometric realizations
[
CK23
, Lemma 2.24]

(6.0.4)

|TX|\cong|X|,

and preserves monomorphisms
[
DKLS24
, Prop.1.31]
,
[
Cis06
, Lem.8.4.29]
.

Lemma 6.0.2
.

Assume that

(6.0.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

is a commutative square in the category of sets and that all the maps are monomorphisms.
If the square is a pushout then it is also a pullback.

Proof.

We can assume that
A\subseteq B,C\subseteq D
and the maps are inclusions. Then the fact that
D
is a pushout means that the map
B\sqcup_{A}C\to D
is a bijection. This implies that
A=B\cap C
.
∎

Lemma 6.0.3
.

Assume that
X
is a cubical set and
U_{0},\dots,U_{n}\subseteq X
are its cubical subsets. Let us identify
TU_{i}
with its image in
TX
. Then

(6.0.6)

T\left(\bigcap_{i=0}^{n}U_{i}\right)=\bigcap_{i=0}^{n}TU_{i}.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the statement for
n=1
, as the general case follows by induction.
Since
T
preserves pushouts and monomorphisms, the square

(6.0.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(U_{0}\cap U_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TU_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TU_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(U_{0}\cup U_{1})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

is a pushout square whose all maps are monomorphisms. Since limits and colimits in
\mathsf{sSet}
are component-wise, using Lemma
6.0.2
, we obtain that this square is a pullback. Therefore
T(U_{0}\cap U_{1})=TU_{0}\cap TU_{1}.

∎

Lemma 6.0.4
.

A map of cubical sets
f
is a weak equivalence if and only if
Tf
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets.

Proof.

It follows from Corollary
2.1.8
and the isomorphism
|Tf|\cong|f|
.
∎

Proposition 6.0.5
.

Let
X
be a cubical set and
\mathcal{U}=(U_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of its cubical subsets such that
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
and
U_{\sigma}
is weakly contractible for any
\sigma\in\mathsf{Ner}(\mathcal{U}).
Then there is a homotopy equivalence

(6.0.8)

|X|\simeq|\mathsf{Ner}(\mathcal{U})|.

(see Definition
4.0.6
for the definition of
\mathsf{Ner}(\mathcal{U})
).

Proof.

This follows from the similar statement for simplicial sets
[
IX24
, Th. 12.6]
, and the fact that
T:\mathsf{cSet}\to\mathsf{sSet}
preserves monomorphisms and intersections (Lemma
6.0.3
) and
|TX|\cong|X|
.
∎

Proposition 6.0.6
.

Let
\varphi:X\to Y
be a morphism of cubical sets and let
\mathcal{U}=(U_{i})_{i\in I}
and
\mathcal{V}=(V_{i})_{i\in I}
be covers of
X
and
Y
respectively such that
\varphi(U_{i})\subseteq V_{i}.
Assume that for any
i_{0},\dots,i_{n}\in I
the map

(6.0.9)

U_{i_{0}}\cap\dots\cap U_{i_{n}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}V_{i_{0}}\cap\dots\cap V_{i_{n}}

is a weak equivalence. Then
\varphi
is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

The statement follows from the similar statement for simplicial sets
[
IX24
, Prop.12.7]
, Lemma
6.0.4
and the fact that
T:\mathsf{cSet}\to\mathsf{sSet}
preserves monomorphisms and intersections (Lemma
6.0.3
) and
|TX|\cong|X|
.
∎
